Irreducible components of affine Deligne-Lusztig varieties
Sian Nie

Abstract
. We determine the (top-dimensional) irreducible components (and their stabilizers in the Frobenius twisted centralizer group) of affine Deligne-Lusztig varieties in the affine Grassmannian of a reductive group, by constructing a natural map from the set of irreducible components to the set of Mirković-Vilonen cycles. This in particular verifies a conjecture by Miaofen Chen and Xinwen Zhu.



Introduction

0.1.
Background

The notion of affine Deligne-Lusztig variety was first introduced by Rapoport in
[
46
]
, which plays an important role in understanding geometric and arithmetic properties of Shimura varieties. Thanks to the uniformization theorem by Rapoport and Zink
[
48
]
, the Newton strata of Shimura varieties can be described explicitly in terms of so-called Rapoport-Zink spaces, whose underlying spaces are special cases of affine Deligne-Lusztig varieties.

In
[
35
]
and
[
46
]
, Kottwitz and Rapoport made several conjectures on basic properties of affine Deligne-Lusztig varieties. Most of them have been verified by a number of authors. We mention the works by Rapoport-Richartz
[
47
]
, Kottwitz
[
34
]
, Gashi
[
7
]
, and He
[
22
]
,
[
24
]
on the “Mazur inequality” criterion of non-emptiness; the works by Görtz-Haines-Kottwitz-Reuman
[
9
]
, He
[
25
]
, He-Yu
[
27
]
, Viehmann
[
51
]
, Hamacher
[
11
]
, and Zhu
[
58
]
on the dimension formula; the works by Hartl-Viehmann
[
19
]
,
[
20
]
, Milićević-Viehmann
[
42
]
, and Hamacher
[
12
]
on the irreducible components; and the works by Viehmann
[
54
]
, Chen-Kisin-Viehmann
[
3
]
, and the author
[
43
]
on the connected components in the hyperspecial case (see also
[
28
]
,
[
4
]
for some partial results in the parahoric case). For a thorough survey we refer to the report
[
23
]
. These advances on affine Deligne-Lusztig varieties have found several interesting applications in arithmetic geometry. For example, the dimension formula leads to a proof by Hamacher
[
12
]
for the Grothendieck conjecture on the closure relations of Newton strata of Shimura varieties, and the description of connected components in
[
3
]
plays an essential role in the proof by Kisin
[
31
]
for the Langlands-Rapoport conjecture on mod
p
points of Shimura varieties (see
[
59
]
,
[
26
]
for recent progresses).

0.2.
Main results

This paper is concerned with the parametrization problem of top-dimensional irreducible components of affine Deligne-Lusztig varieties. The problem was first considered by Xiao and Zhu in
[
56
]
, where they solved the unramified case in order to prove certain cases of the Tate conjecture for Shimura varieties. We will provide a complete parametrization in the general case.

To state the results, we introduce some notations. Let
F
be a non-archimedean local field with residue field
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. Let
{\breve{F}}
be the completion of the maximal unramified extension of
F
. Denote by
\mathcal{O}_{F}
and
\mathcal{O}_{\breve{F}}
the valuation rings of
F
and
{\breve{F}}
respectively. Let
\sigma
be the Frobenius automorphism of
{\breve{F}}/F
.

Let
G
be a connected reductive group over
\mathcal{O}_{F}
. Fix
T\subseteq B\subseteq G
, where
T
is a maximal torus and
B=TU
is a Borel subgroup with unipotent radical
U
. Denote by
Y
the cocharacter group of
T
, and by
Y^{+}
the set of dominant cocharacters determined by
B
. Let
K=G(\mathcal{O}_{\breve{F}})
. Fix a uniformizer
t\in\mathcal{O}_{F}
and set
t^{\lambda}={\lambda}(t)\in G({\breve{F}})
for
{\lambda}\in Y
. Then we have the Cartan decomposition for the affine Grassmannian

{\rm Gr}={\rm Gr}_{G}=G({\breve{F}})/K=\sqcup_{\mu\in Y^{+}}{\rm Gr}_{\mu}^{\circ},

where
{\rm Gr}_{\mu}^{\circ}=Kt^{\mu}K/K
. For
b\in G({\breve{F}})
and
\mu\in Y^{+}
, the attached affine Deligne-Lusztig set is defined by

X_{\mu}(b)=X_{\mu}^{G}(b)=\{g\in G({\breve{F}});g^{-1}b\sigma(g)\in Kt^{\mu}K\}/K,

which is a subscheme locally of finite type in the usual sense if
{\rm char}(F)>0
, and in the sense of Bhatt-Scholze
[
2
]
and Zhu
[
58
]
if
{\rm char}(F)=0
. By left multiplication it carries an action of the group

\mathbb{J}_{b}=\mathbb{J}_{b}^{G}=\{g\in G({\breve{F}});g^{-1}b\sigma(g)=b\}.

Up to isomorphism,
X_{\mu}(b)
only depends on the
\sigma
-conjugacy class
[b]=[b]_{G}
of
b
. Thanks to Kottwitz
[
32
]
,
[b]
is uniquely determined by two invariants: the Kottwitz point
\kappa_{G}(b)\in\pi_{1}(G)_{\sigma}=\pi_{1}(G)/(1-\sigma)(\pi_{1}(G))
and the Newton point
\nu_{G}(b)\in Y_{\mathbb{R}}=Y\otimes\mathbb{R}
, see
[
18
, §2.1]
. Then
X_{\mu}(b)\neq\emptyset
if and only if
\kappa_{G}(t^{\mu})=\kappa_{G}(b)
and
\nu_{G}(b)\leqslant\mu^{\diamond}
, where
\mu^{\diamond}
denotes the
\sigma
-average of
\mu
, and
\leqslant
denotes the partial order on
Y_{\mathbb{R}}
such that
v\leqslant v^{\prime}\in Y_{\mathbb{R}}
if
v^{\prime}-v
is a non-negative linear combination of coroots in
B
. Moreover, in this case, its dimension is given by

\dim X_{\mu}(b)=\langle\rho_{G},\mu-\nu_{G}(b)\rangle-\frac{1}{2}{\rm def}_{G}(b),

where
\rho_{G}
is the half-sum of roots of
B
and
{\rm def}_{G}(b)
is the
defect
of
b
, see
[
33
, §1.9.1]
. Let
{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu}(b)
denote the set of top-dimensional irreducible components of
X_{\mu}(b)
.

The first goal of this paper is to give an explicit description of the set
\mathbb{J}_{b}\backslash{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu}(b)
of
\mathbb{J}_{b}
-orbits of
{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu}(b)
. We invoke a conjecture by Xinwen Zhu and Miaofen Chen which suggests a parametrization of
\mathbb{J}_{b}\backslash{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu}(b)
by certain Mirković-Vilonen cycles.

Recall that Mirković-Vilonen cycles are irreducible components of
S^{\lambda}\cap{\rm Gr}_{\mu}
for
\mu\in Y^{+}
and
{\lambda}\in Y
, where
S^{\lambda}=U({\breve{F}})t^{\lambda}K/K
and
{\rm Gr}_{\mu}=\overline{{\rm Gr}_{\mu}^{\circ}}
. We write
{\rm MV}_{\mu}=\sqcup_{\lambda}{\rm MV}_{\mu}({\lambda})
with
{\rm MV}_{\mu}({\lambda})={\rm Irr}(S^{\lambda}\cap{\rm Gr}_{\mu})
the set of irreducible components.

Let
\widehat{G}
be the Langlands dual of
G
defined over
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{l}
with
l\neq{\rm char}(\mathbb{k})
. Denote by
V_{\mu}=V_{\mu}^{\widehat{G}}
the irreducible
\widehat{G}
-module of highest weight
\mu
. The crystal basis (or the canonical basis)
\mathbb{B}_{\mu}=\mathbb{B}_{\mu}^{\widehat{G}}
of
V_{\mu}
was first constructed by Lusztig
[
39
]
and Kashiwara
[
30
]
. In
[
1
, Theorem 3.1]
, Braverman and Gaitsgory proved that the set
{\rm MV}_{\mu}
of Mirković-Vilonen cycles admits a
\widehat{G}
-crystal structure and gives rise to a crystal basis of
V_{\mu}
via the geometric Satake isomorphism
[
41
]
. In
[
56
, §3.3]
, Xiao and Zhu constructed a canonical isomorphism
\mathbb{B}_{\mu}\cong{\rm MV}_{\mu}
using Littelmann’s path model
[
38
]
, which we denote by
\delta\mapsto S_{\delta}
. The advantage of using
\mathbb{B}_{\mu}
is that its
\widehat{G}
-crystal structure is given in a combinatorial way.

In
[
18
, §2.1]
, Hamacher and Viehmann proved that, under the partial order
\leqslant
, there is a unique maximal element
{\underline{\lambda}}_{G}(b)
in the set

\{{\underline{\lambda}}\in Y_{\sigma}=Y/(1-\sigma)Y;{\underline{\lambda}}=\kappa_{G}(b),{\underline{\lambda}}^{\diamond}\leqslant\nu_{G}(b)\},

which is called “the best integral approximation” of
\nu_{G}(b)
. Let
V_{\mu}({\underline{\lambda}}_{G}(b))
be the sum of
{\lambda}
-weight spaces
V_{\mu}({\lambda})
with
{\lambda}={\underline{\lambda}}_{G}(b)\in Y_{\sigma}
, whose basis in
\mathbb{B}_{\mu}
and
{\rm MV}_{\mu}
is denoted by
\mathbb{B}_{\mu}({\underline{\lambda}}_{G}(b))
and
{\rm MV}_{\mu}({\underline{\lambda}}_{G}(b))
respectively.

Conjecture 0.1

(Chen-Zhu)
.

There exist natural bijections

\mathbb{J}_{b}\backslash{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu}(b)\cong{\rm MV}_{\mu}({\underline{\lambda}}_{G}(b))\cong\mathbb{B}_{\mu}({\underline{\lambda}}_{G}(b)).

In particular,
|\mathbb{J}_{b}\backslash{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu}(b)|=\dim V_{\mu}({\underline{\lambda}}_{G}(b))
.

Remark 0.1
.

If
{\rm char}(F)>0
,
X_{\mu}(b)
is equi-dimensional by
[
20
]
and
{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu}(b)
coincides with the set of irreducible components of
X_{\mu}(b)
. If
{\rm char}(F)=0
, the equi-dimensionality of
X_{\mu}(b)
is not fully established, see
[
18
, Theorem 3.4]
. However,
X_{\mu}(b)
is always equi-dimensional if
\mu
is minuscule.

Remark 0.2
.

If
\mu
is minuscule and either
G
is split or
b
is superbasic, Conjecture
6.7
is proved by Hamacher and Viehmann
[
18
]
using the method of semi-modules, which originates in the work
[
6
]
by de Jong and Oort. If
b
is unramified, that is,
{\rm def}_{G}(b)=0
, it is proved by Xiao and Zhu
[
56
]
using the geometric Satake. In both cases, the authors obtained complete descriptions of
{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu}(b)
.

Remark 0.3
.

A complete description of
{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu}(b)
was also known for the case where
G
is
{\rm GL}_{n}
or
{\rm GSp}_{2n}
and
\mu
is minuscule, see
[
52
]
and
[
53
]
.

Remark 0.4
.

If the pair
(G,\mu)
is
fully Hodge-Newton decomposable
(see
[
10
]
),
X_{\mu}(b)
admits a nice stratification by classical Deligne-Lusztig varieties, whose index set and closure relations are encoded in the Bruhat-Tits building of
\mathbb{J}_{b}
. Such a stratification has important applications in arithmetic geometry, including the Kudla-Rapoport program
[
36
]
,
[
37
]
and Zhang’s Arithmetic Fundamental Lemma
[
57
]
. We mention the works by Vollaard-Wedhorn
[
55
]
, Rapoport-Terstiege-Wilson
[
49
]
, Howard-Pappas
[
16
]
,
[
17
]
, and Görtz-He
[
8
]
for some of the typical examples.

Let
I\subseteq G({\breve{F}})
be the standard Iwahori subgroup associated to the triple
T\subseteq B\subseteq G
, see §
1.1
. By Proposition
1.2
, for
b\in G({\breve{F}})
, there is a unique standard Levi subgroup
T\subseteq M\subseteq G
and a
superbasic
element
b_{M}
of
M({\breve{F}})
, unique up to
M({\breve{F}})
-
\sigma
-conjugation, such that
[b_{M}]=[b]
and
\nu_{M}(b_{M})=\nu_{G}(b)
. Moreover, we may and do choose
b_{M}
such that
b_{M}T({\breve{F}})b_{M}^{-1}=T({\breve{F}})
and
b_{M}I_{M}b_{M}^{-1}=I_{M}
, where
I_{M}=M({\breve{F}})\cap I
is the standard Iwahori subgroup of
M({\breve{F}})
. Take
b=b_{M}
. Let
P=MN
be the standard parabolic subgroup with
N\subseteq U
its unipotent radical. Using the Iwasawa decomposition
G({\breve{F}})=N({\breve{F}})M({\breve{F}})K
we have

{\rm Gr}=N({\breve{F}})M({\breve{F}})K/K=\sqcup_{{\lambda}\in Y}N({\breve{F}})I_{M}t^{\lambda}K/K.

For
{\lambda}\in Y
let
\theta^{P}_{\lambda}:N({\breve{F}})I_{M}\to{\rm Gr}
be the map given by
h\mapsto ht^{\lambda}K
.

Our first goal is to prove Conjecture
6.7
.

Theorem 0.5
.

Let
b
and
M
be as above. Then there exists a map

\gamma=\gamma^{G}:{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu}(b)\to\mathbb{B}_{\mu}({\underline{\lambda}}_{G}(b))

such that for
C\in{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu}(b)
we have

\overline{\{(ht^{\lambda})^{-1}b\sigma(ht^{\lambda})K;h\in(\theta^{P}_{\lambda})^{-1}(C)\}}=\epsilon_{\lambda}^{M}\overline{S_{\gamma(C)}},

where
{\lambda}
is the unique cocharacter such that
N({\breve{F}})I_{M}t^{\lambda}K/K\cap C
is open dense in
C
and
\epsilon_{\lambda}^{M}
is certain Weyl group element for
M
associated to
{\lambda}
(see §
1.5
). Moreover,
\gamma
factors through a bijection

\mathbb{J}_{b}\backslash{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu}(b)\cong\mathbb{B}_{\mu}({\underline{\lambda}}_{G}(b)).

Remark 0.6
.

The equality
|\mathbb{J}_{b}\backslash{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu}(b)|=\dim V_{\mu}({\underline{\lambda}}_{G}(b))
, which is the numerical version of Conjecture
6.7
, is proved by Rong Zhou and Yihang Zhu in
[
60
]
(even for the quasi-split case), and by the author in an earlier version of this paper, using different approaches.

It is a remarkable feature that the tensor product of two crystals bases is again a crystal basis. So there is a natural map

\otimes:\mathbb{B}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{\widehat{G}^{d}}=\mathbb{B}_{\mu_{1}}^{\widehat{G}}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{B}_{\mu_{d}}^{\widehat{G}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{B}_{\mu_{1}}^{\widehat{G}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbb{B}_{\mu_{d}}^{\widehat{G}}\longrightarrow\sqcup_{\mu}\mathbb{B}_{\mu}^{\widehat{G}}({\underline{\lambda}}_{G}(b)),

where the first map is given by taking the tensor product, and the second one is the canonical projection to highest weight
\widehat{G}
-crystals.

On the other hand, there is also a “tensor structure” among affine Deligne-Lusztig varieties coming from the geometric Satake. Consider the product
G^{d}
of
d
copies of
G
together with a Frobenius automorphism given by

(g_{1},g_{2},\dots,g_{d})\mapsto(g_{2},\dots,g_{d},\sigma(g_{1})).

For
{\mu_{\bullet}}=(\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{d})\in Y^{d}
and
{b_{\bullet}}=(1,\dots,1,b)\in G^{d}({\breve{F}})
with
b\in G({\breve{F}})
, we can define the affine Deligne-Lusztig variety
X_{\mu_{\bullet}}({b_{\bullet}})
in a similar way. We know (see Corollary
1.6
) that the projection
{\rm Gr}^{d}\to{\rm Gr}
to the first factor induces a map

{\rm pr}:{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu_{\bullet}}({b_{\bullet}})\to\sqcup_{\mu}{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu}(b),

which serves as the functor of taking tensor products.

Our second main result shows that the map
\gamma
(for various
G
) preserves the tensor structures on both sides.

Theorem 0.7
.

There is a Cartesian square

\textstyle{{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu_{\bullet}}({b_{\bullet}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm pr}}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 8.19447pt\ \gamma^{G^{d}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{B}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{\widehat{G}^{d}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\otimes}
\textstyle{\sqcup_{\mu}{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu}(b)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 8.19447pt\ \gamma^{G}}
\textstyle{\sqcup_{\mu}\mathbb{B}_{\mu}^{\widehat{G}}.}

As a consequence, if
\mathbb{B}_{\mu}^{\widehat{G}}
appears in the tensor product
\mathbb{B}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{\widehat{G}}=\mathbb{B}_{\mu_{1}}^{\widehat{G}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbb{B}_{\mu_{d}}^{\widehat{G}}
, then
\gamma^{G}
is determined by
\gamma^{G^{d}}
and
{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu}(b)={\rm pr}((\otimes\circ\gamma^{G^{d}})^{-1}(\mathbb{B}_{\mu}^{\widehat{G}}))
.

Remark 0.8
.

The map
\gamma^{G}
coincides with the natural constructions of
[
56
]
and
[
18
]
for
quasi-minuscule
cocharacters, see
[
45
, Lemme 1.1]
. On the other hand, we know that each highest weight module appears in some tensor product of quasi-minuscule highest weight modules. Thus Theorem
0.7
gives a characterization of the map
\gamma^{G}
by the tensor structure of
\widehat{G}
-crystals.

Remark 0.9
.

As an essential application, Theorem
0.7
, combined with the construction of
[
18
]
, provides a representation-theoretic construction of
{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu}(b)
up to taking closures. Indeed, by the reduction method in
[
9
, §5]
, it suffices to consider the case where
b
is superbasic and
G={\rm Res}_{E/F}{\rm GL}_{n}
with
E/F
a finite unramified extension. In this case, we can choose a minuscule cocharacter
{\mu_{\bullet}}\in Y^{d}
for some
d
such that
\mathbb{B}_{\mu}^{\widehat{G}}
appears in
\mathbb{B}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{\widehat{G}}
. As
{\mu_{\bullet}}
is minuscule, both
{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu_{\bullet}}({b_{\bullet}})
and
\gamma^{G^{d}}
are explicitly constructed in
[
18
]
. Then Theorem
0.7
shows how to obtain
{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu}(b)
from
{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu_{\bullet}}({b_{\bullet}})
by taking the projection
{\rm pr}
. The key is to decompose the tensor product into simple objects

\mathbb{B}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{\widehat{G}}=\mathbb{B}_{\mu_{1}}^{\widehat{G}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbb{B}_{\mu_{d}}^{\widehat{G}}=\sqcup_{\mu}(\mathbb{B}_{\mu}^{\widehat{G}})^{m_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{\mu}},

which can be solved combinatorially using the “Littlewood-Richardson” rule for
\widehat{G}
-crystals (see
[
38
, §10]
). Here
m_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{\mu}
denotes the multiplicity with which
\mathbb{B}_{\mu}^{\widehat{G}}
appears in
\mathbb{B}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{\widehat{G}}
.

Remark 0.10
.

In the case mentioned above where
G={\rm Res}_{E/F}{\rm GL}_{n}
and
b
is superbasic, Viehmann
[
51
]
and Hamacher
[
11
]
defined a decomposition of
X_{\mu}(b)
using extended semi-modules (or extended EL-charts). In particular,
\mathbb{J}_{b}\backslash{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu}(b)
is parameterized by the set of equivalence classes of
top
extended semi-modules, that is, the semi-modules whose corresponding strata are top-dimensional. However, it unclear how to construct all the top extended semi-modules if
\mu
is non-minuscule. It would be interesting to give an explicit correspondence between the top extended semi-modules and the crystal elements in
\mathbb{B}_{\mu}({\underline{\lambda}}_{G}(b))
.

The third goal is to give an explicit construction of an irreducible component from each
\mathbb{J}_{b}
-orbit of
{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu}(b)
and compute its stabilizer. Combined with Theorem
0.5
, this will provide a complete parametrization of
{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu}(b)
in theory. If
b
is unramified, this task has been done by Xiao-Zhu
[
56
]
. Otherwise, using Theorem
0.7
, it suffices to consider the case where
G
is adjoint,
\mu
is minuscule, and
b
is basic. To handle this case, we consider the decomposition

X_{\mu}(b)=\sqcup_{{\lambda}\in Y}X_{\mu}^{\lambda}(b),

where each piece
X_{\mu}^{\lambda}(b)=It^{\lambda}K/K\cap X_{\mu}(b)
is a locally closed subset of
X_{\mu}(b)
.

Theorem 0.11
.

Keep the assumptions on
G,b,\mu
as above.

(1)
\overline{X_{\mu}^{\lambda}(b)}\in{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu}(b)
if and only if
{\lambda}\in Y
is small;

(2) each
\mathbb{J}_{b}
-orbit of
{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu}(b)
has a representative of the form
\overline{X_{\mu}^{\lambda}(b)}
with
{\lambda}
small;

(3) if
{\lambda}\in Y
is small, then the stabilizer of
\overline{X_{\mu}^{\lambda}(b)}
in
\mathbb{J}_{b}
is the standard parahoric subgroup of type
\Pi({\lambda})
, which is of maximal volume among all parahoric subgroups of
\mathbb{J}_{b}
.

We refer to §
6.4
for the meanings of the smallness of
{\lambda}
and the associated type
\Pi({\lambda})
. As a consequence, we obtain the following result without restrictions on
G
,
b
, and
\mu
.

Theorem 0.12

(He-Zhou-Zhu)
.

The stabilizer of each top-dimensional irreducible component of
X_{\mu}(b)
in
\mathbb{J}_{b}
is a parahoric subgroup of maximal volume.

Remark 0.13
.

Theorem
0.12
is first proved by He-Zhou-Zhu
[
29
]
. It is also verified in
[
29
]
that a parahoric subgroup has maximal volume if and only if its Weyl group has
maximal length
(see Theorem
6.4
). This gives an explicit characterization of parahoric subgroups of maximal volume by their types.

The original proof in
[
29
]
is based on the twisted orbital integral method (see
[
60
]
) and the Deligne-Lusztig reduction method (see
[
24
]
). Our proof is based on the combinatorial properties of small cocharacters, which shows that the stabilizers are parahoric subgroups of maximal length.

0.3.
Strategy

Now we briefly discuss the strategy. First we reduced the problem to the case where
b
is basic and
G
is simple and adjoint. If
G
has no non-zero minuscule coweights, then
b
is unramified and the problem has been solved by Xiao-Zhu
[
56
]
. Thus, it remains to consider the case where
G
has some non-zero minuscule cocharacter. In particular, any irreducible
\hat{G}
-module appears in some tensor product of irreducible
\hat{G}
-modules with minuscule highest weights (see Lemma
4.6
). Combined with the geometric Satake, this observation enables us to decompose the problem into three ingredients: (1) the construction of
\gamma
in the case where
b
is superbasic; (2) the equality

|\mathbb{J}_{b}\backslash{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu}(b)|=\dim V_{\mu}({\underline{\lambda}}_{G}(b))

in the case where
\mu
is minuscule and
b
is basic; and (3) the construction of irreducible components and the computation of their stabilizers in the situation of (2).

The first ingredient is solved in §
3
by combining the semi-module method and Littelmann’s path model. For the second ingredients, we consider in §
5
the following decomposition

X_{\mu}(b)=\sqcup_{{\lambda}\in Y}X_{\mu}^{\lambda}(b).

In Proposition
2.9
, we show that
\mathbb{J}_{b}
acts on
{\rm Irr}X_{\mu}^{\lambda}(b)
transitively and

{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu}(b)=\sqcup_{{\lambda}\in\mathcal{A}_{\mu,b}^{\rm top}}{\rm Irr}\overline{X_{\mu}^{\lambda}(b)},

where
\mathcal{A}_{\mu,b}^{\rm top}
is the set of coweights
{\lambda}
such that
\dim X_{\mu}^{\lambda}(b)=\dim X_{\mu}(b)
. In particular, the action of
\mathbb{J}_{b}
on
{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu}(b)
induces an equivalence relation on
\mathcal{A}_{\mu,b}^{\rm top}
, and the
\mathbb{J}_{b}
-orbits of
{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu}(b)
are naturally parameterized by the corresponding equivalence classes of
\mathcal{A}_{\mu,b}^{\rm top}
. Therefore, it remains to show the number of these equivalence classes is equal to
\dim V_{\mu}({\underline{\lambda}}_{G}(b))
. To this end, we give an explicit description of
\mathcal{A}_{\mu,b}^{\rm top}
(see Proposition
5.12
) and reduce the question to the superbasic case, which has been solved by Hamacher-Viehmann
[
18
, Theorem 1.5]
. Finally, to solve the last ingredient, we introduce the notion of small cocharacters in §
6
. We prove that
X_{\mu}^{\lambda}(b)
is irreducible if and only if
{\lambda}
is small, and show its stabilizer is of maximal length in this case. Here we will use a general result
[
60
, Theorem 3.3.1]
by Zhou-Zhu showing that the stabilizers are parahoric subgroups, which simplifies the original proof following
[
56
]
.

Remark 0.14
.

Even if the simple adjoint group
G
has no non-zero minuscule cocharacters, the above approach still works but is more technically involved, by using quasi-minuscule cocharacters instead.

0.4.
Comparison with the work
[
60
]
by Zhou-Zhu

As mentioned before, this paper aims to give a complete parametrization of
{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu}(b)
, which consists of three parts: the parametrization of
\mathbb{J}_{b}\backslash{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu}(b)
; the construction of representative irreducible components; and the computation of their stabilizers. The major overlap with
[
60
]
lies in the first part, see Remark
0.6
. A key new feature of this paper is that the
\widehat{G}
-crystal structure plays an essential role in the construction, see Remark
0.8
&
0.9
. This in particular enables us to handle the type
A
case, which is not covered in
[
60
]
. There is a minor overlap in the third part, where the difference is that this paper gives an algorithm for computing the stabilizers (see Theorem
0.11
); while the work by Zhou-Zhu produces extra interesting information on the volumes of stabilizers, see
[
60
, Theorem C & Remark 1.4.3]
.

1.
Preliminaries

We keep the notations in the introduction. Set
K_{H}=H(\mathcal{O}_{\breve{F}})
for any subgroup
H\subseteq G
over
\mathcal{O}_{\breve{F}}
.

1.1.
Root system

Let
\mathcal{R}=(Y,\Phi^{\vee},X,\Phi,\mathbb{S}_{0})
be the based root datum of
G
associated to the triple
T\subseteq B\subseteq G
, where
X
and
Y
denote the (absolute) character and cocharacter groups of
T
respectively together with a perfect pairing
\langle,\rangle:X\times Y\to\mathbb{Z}
;
\Phi
(resp.
\Phi^{\vee}
) is the roots system (resp. coroot system);
\mathbb{S}_{0}
is the set of simple reflections. Denote by
\Phi^{+}
the set of (positive) roots appearing in
B
. Then
\Phi=\Phi^{+}\sqcup\Phi^{-}
with
\Phi^{-}=-\Phi^{+}
. For
\alpha\in\Phi
, we denote by
s_{\alpha}
the reflection which sends
{\lambda}\in Y
to
{\lambda}-\langle\alpha,{\lambda}\rangle\alpha^{\vee}
with
\alpha^{\vee}\in\Phi^{\vee}
the corresponding coroot of
\alpha
. The Frobenius map of
G
induces an automorphism of
\mathcal{R}
of finite order, which is still denoted by
\sigma
. In particular,
\sigma
acts on
Y_{\mathbb{R}}
as a linear transformation of finite order.

Let
W_{0}=W_{G}
be the Weyl group of
T
in
G
, which is a reflection subgroup of
{\rm GL}(Y_{\mathbb{R}})
generated by
\mathbb{S}_{0}
. The Iwahori-Weyl group of
T
in
G
is given by

\tilde{W}=\tilde{W}_{G}=N_{T}({\breve{F}})/K_{T}\cong Y\rtimes W_{0}=\{t^{\lambda}w;{\lambda}\in Y,w\in W_{0}\},

where
N_{T}
denotes the normalizer of
T
in
G
. We can embed
\tilde{W}
into the group of affine transformations of
Y_{\mathbb{R}}
so that the action of
{\tilde{w}}=t^{\mu}w
is given by
v\mapsto\mu+w(v)
. Let
\Phi^{+}
be the set of (positive) roots appearing in Borel subgroup
B\supseteq T
and let

\Delta=\Delta_{G}=\{v\in Y_{\mathbb{R}};0<\langle\alpha,v\rangle<1,\alpha\in\Phi^{+}\}

be the base alcove. Then we have
\tilde{W}=W^{a}\rtimes\Omega
, where
W^{a}=\mathbb{Z}\Phi^{\vee}\rtimes W_{0}
is the affine Weyl group and
\Omega
is the stabilizer of
\Delta
. Let
Y^{+}
be the set of dominant cocharacters. For
\chi,\eta\in Y
we write
\chi\leqslant\eta
if
\eta-\chi
is a sum of positive roots. Write
\chi\leq\eta
if
\bar{\chi}\leqslant\bar{\eta}
. Here
\bar{\eta},\bar{\chi}
are the dominant
W_{0}
-conjugate of
\eta,\chi
respectively.

For
\alpha\in\Phi
, let
U_{\alpha}\subseteq G
denote the corresponding root subgroup. We set

I=K_{T}\prod_{\alpha\in\Phi^{+}}U_{\alpha}(t\mathcal{O}_{{\breve{F}}})\prod_{\beta\in\Phi^{+}}U_{-\beta}(\mathcal{O}_{{\breve{F}}})\subseteq G({\breve{F}}),

which is called the standard Iwahori subgroup associated to
T\subseteq B\subseteq G
. We have the Iwasawa decomposition
G({\breve{F}})=\sqcup_{{\tilde{w}}\in\tilde{W}}I{\tilde{w}}I
.

1.2.
Affine roots

Let
\tilde{\Phi}=\tilde{\Phi}_{G}=\Phi\times\mathbb{Z}
be the set of (real) affine roots. Let
a=\alpha+k:=(\alpha,k)\in\tilde{\Phi}
. Denote by
U_{a}:\mathcal{O}_{{\breve{F}}}\to G({\breve{F}})
,
z\mapsto U_{\alpha}(zt^{k})
the corresponding one-parameter affine root subgroup. We can view
a
as an affine function such that
a(v)=-\langle\alpha,v\rangle+k
for
v\in Y_{\mathbb{R}}
, whose zero locus
H_{a}=\{v\in Y_{\mathbb{R}};a(v)=0\}
is called an affine root hyperplane. Let
s_{a}=s_{H_{a}}=t^{k\alpha^{\vee}}s_{\alpha}\in\tilde{W}
denote the corresponding affine reflection. Set
\tilde{\Phi}^{+}=\{a\in\tilde{\Phi};a(\Delta)>0\}
. Then
\tilde{\Phi}=\tilde{\Phi}^{+}\sqcup\tilde{\Phi}^{-}
with
\tilde{\Phi}^{-}=-\tilde{\Phi}^{+}
. The associated length function
\ell:\tilde{W}\to\mathbb{N}
is defined by
\ell({\tilde{w}})=|\tilde{\Phi}^{-}\cap{\tilde{w}}(\tilde{\Phi}^{+})|
. Let
\mathbb{S}^{a}=\{s_{a};a\in\tilde{\Phi},\ell(s_{a})=1\}
. Then
W^{a}
is generated by
\mathbb{S}^{a}
and
(W^{a},\mathbb{S}^{a})
is a Coxeter system.

Let
\alpha\in\Phi
. Define
\tilde{\alpha}=(\alpha,0)\in\tilde{\Phi}^{+}
if
\alpha<0
and
\tilde{\alpha}=(\alpha,1)\in\tilde{\Phi}^{+}
otherwise. Then the map
\alpha\mapsto\tilde{\alpha}
gives an embedding of
\Phi
into
\tilde{\Phi}^{+}
, whose image is
\{a\in\tilde{\Phi};0<a(\Delta)<1\}
. Let
\Pi
be the set of roots
\alpha\in\Phi
such that
\tilde{\alpha}
is a simple affine root, namely,
\Pi
consists of minus simple roots and highest positive roots.

Lemma 1.1
.

Let
{\tilde{w}},{\tilde{w}}^{\prime}\in\tilde{W}
. Then
I{\tilde{w}}I{\tilde{w}}^{\prime}I\subseteq\cup_{x\leq{\tilde{w}}}Ix{\tilde{w}}^{\prime}I
and
I{\tilde{w}}I{\tilde{w}}^{\prime}I\subseteq\cup_{x^{\prime}\leq{\tilde{w}}^{\prime}}I{\tilde{w}}x^{\prime}I
. Consequently,
{\tilde{w}}It^{\lambda}K\subseteq\cup_{x\leq{\tilde{w}}}It^{x({\lambda})}K
for
{\lambda}\in Y
. Here
\leq
is the usual Bruhat order on
\tilde{W}
associated to
\ell
.

1.3.
Levi subgroup

Let
M\supseteq T
be a (semistandard) Levi subgroup of
G
. By replacing the triple
T\subseteq B\subseteq G
with
T\subseteq B\cap M\subseteq M
, we can define
\Phi_{M}^{\pm}
,
\tilde{W}_{M}
,
W_{M}^{a}
,
W_{M}
,
I_{M}
,
\tilde{\Phi}_{M}^{\pm}
,
\Delta_{M}
,
\Omega_{M}
and so on as above. For
v\in Y_{\mathbb{R}}
, we denote by
M_{v}
the Levi subgroup generated by
T
and
U_{\alpha}
for
\alpha\in\Phi
such that
\langle\alpha,v\rangle=0
, and denote by
N_{v}
the unipotent subgroup generated by
U_{\beta}
for
\beta\in\Phi
such that
\langle\beta,v\rangle>0
. We say
M
is standard if
M=M_{v}
for some dominant vector
v\in Y^{+}
.

1.4.
Superbasic element

We say
b\in G({\breve{F}})
is superbasic if none of its
\sigma
-conjugates is contained in a proper Levi subgroup of
G
. In particular,
b
is basic in
G({\breve{F}})
, that is, the Newton point
\nu_{G}(b)
is central for
\Phi
.

Proposition 1.2
.

If
b\in G({\breve{F}})
is basic, then there exists a unique standard Levi subgroup
M\subseteq G
such that
M({\breve{F}})\cap[b]
is a (single) superbasic
\sigma
-conjugacy class of
M({\breve{F}})
.

The existence is known. The uniqueness is proved in Appendix B, which is only used in the formulation of Theorem
0.5
.

1.5.
The element
\epsilon_{\lambda}^{G}

Let
{\lambda}\in Y
and
\gamma\in\Phi
. We set
{\lambda}_{\gamma}=-\tilde{\gamma}({\lambda})
, that is,
{\lambda}_{\gamma}=\langle\gamma,{\lambda}\rangle
if
\gamma<0
and
{\lambda}_{\gamma}=\langle\gamma,{\lambda}\rangle-1
otherwise. Let
U_{\lambda}
(resp.
U_{\lambda}^{-}
) be the (maximal) unipotent subgroup of
G
generated by
U_{\alpha}
such that
{\lambda}_{\alpha}\geqslant 0
(resp.
{\lambda}_{\alpha}<0
). We define
\epsilon_{\lambda}=\epsilon^{G}_{\lambda}\in W_{0}
such that
U_{\lambda}={}^{\epsilon_{\lambda}}U:=\epsilon_{\lambda}U\epsilon_{\lambda}^{-1}
. Here
U
denotes the unipotent radical of
B
. Set
I_{\lambda}=I\cap t^{{\lambda}}Kt^{-{\lambda}}
and

\displaystyle I_{\lambda}^{-}

\displaystyle=K_{T}(I_{\lambda}\cap U_{\lambda}^{-})=K_{T}(I\cap U_{\lambda}^{-});

\displaystyle I_{\lambda}^{+}

\displaystyle=K_{T}(I_{\lambda}\cap U_{\lambda})=K_{T}t^{\lambda}K_{U_{\lambda}}t^{-{\lambda}}.

It follows from the Iwasawa decomposition that
I_{\lambda}=I_{\lambda}^{-}I_{\lambda}^{+}=I_{\lambda}^{+}I_{\lambda}^{-}
.

Let
p:\tilde{W}\rtimes\langle\sigma\rangle\to W_{0}\rtimes\langle\sigma\rangle
denote the natural projection, where
\langle\sigma\rangle
is the finite cyclic subgroup of
{\rm GL}(Y_{\mathbb{R}})
generated by
\sigma
.

Lemma 1.3
.

Let
{\lambda}\in Y
and
\alpha\in\Phi
. Then

(1)
{\lambda}_{\alpha}+{\lambda}_{-\alpha}=-1
;

(2)
s_{{\tilde{\alpha}}}({\lambda})={\lambda}-{\lambda}_{\alpha}\alpha^{\vee}
;

(3)
{\lambda}_{\alpha}=\omega({\lambda})_{p(\omega)(\alpha)}
and
\epsilon_{\omega({\lambda})}=p(\omega)\epsilon_{\lambda}
for
\omega\in\Omega
.

Proof.

The first two statements follow directly by definition. We show the last one. Write
\omega=t^{\eta}p(\omega)
for some
\eta\in Y
. Then

\langle p(\omega)(\alpha),\omega({\lambda})\rangle=\langle\alpha,{\lambda}\rangle+\langle p(\omega)(\alpha),\eta\rangle.

By the statement (1) we may assume
\alpha>0
. Since
\omega\in\Omega
,
\langle p(\omega)(\alpha),\eta\rangle=0
if
p(\omega)(\alpha)>0
and
\langle p(\omega)(\alpha),\eta\rangle=-1
otherwise. It follows that
\omega({\lambda})_{p(\omega)(\alpha)}={\lambda}_{\alpha}
. In particular,
U_{\omega({\lambda})}={}^{p(\omega)}U_{\lambda}
and hence
\epsilon_{\omega({\lambda})}=p(\omega)\epsilon_{\lambda}
.
∎

Lemma 1.4
.

Let
{\lambda},\eta\in Y
such that
\eta-{\lambda}
is minuscule. Then
I_{\eta}^{-}\subseteq I_{\lambda}
.

Proof.

It suffices to show
U_{\alpha}(t^{\epsilon_{\alpha}}\mathcal{O}_{{\breve{F}}})\subseteq I_{\lambda}
for
{\lambda}_{\alpha}<0
, where
\epsilon_{\alpha}=0
if
\alpha<0
and
\epsilon_{\alpha}=1
otherwise. If
{\lambda}_{\alpha}<0
, there is nothing to prove. Suppose
{\lambda}_{\alpha}>0
. Then
\langle\alpha,{\lambda}\rangle>\langle\alpha,\eta\rangle
and hence
\langle\alpha,{\lambda}\rangle=\langle\alpha,\eta\rangle+1
as
\eta-{\lambda}
is minuscule. This means
-1\leqslant\langle\alpha,\eta\rangle\leqslant 0
. If
\langle\alpha,\eta\rangle=0
, then
\alpha>0
(since
{\lambda}_{\alpha}<0
) and
U_{\alpha}(t^{\epsilon_{\alpha}}\mathcal{O}_{{\breve{F}}})=U_{\alpha}(t\mathcal{O}_{{\breve{F}}})=U_{\alpha}(t^{\langle\alpha,{\lambda}\rangle}\mathcal{O}_{{\breve{F}}})\subseteq I_{\lambda}^{+}
. If
\langle\alpha,\eta\rangle=-1
, then
\alpha<0
(since
{\lambda}_{\alpha}>0
) and
U_{\alpha}(t^{\epsilon_{\alpha}}\mathcal{O}_{{\breve{F}}})=U_{\alpha}(\mathcal{O}_{{\breve{F}}})=U_{\alpha}(t^{\langle\alpha,{\lambda}\rangle}\mathcal{O}_{{\breve{F}}})\subseteq I_{\lambda}^{+}
.
∎

1.6.
The convolution map

Let
d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 1}
and let
G^{d}
be the product of
d
copies of
G
. Let
\sigma_{\bullet}
be the Frobenius-type automorphism on
G^{d}
given by
(g_{1},g_{2},\dots,g_{d})\mapsto(g_{2},\dots,g_{d},\sigma(g_{1}))
. We set
{b_{\bullet}}=(1,\dots,1,b)\in G({\breve{F}})^{d}
. Let
{\mu_{\bullet}}=(\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{d})\in Y^{d}
be a dominant cocharacter of
G^{d}
. Let
X_{\mu_{\bullet}}({b_{\bullet}})
be the corresponding affine Deligne-Lusztig variety in
{\rm Gr}^{d}
using automorphism
\sigma_{\bullet}
. Consider the twisted product

{\rm Gr}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{\circ}:=Kt^{\mu_{1}}K\times_{K}\cdots\times_{K}Kt^{\mu_{d}}K/K

together with the convolution map

m_{\mu_{\bullet}}:{\rm Gr}_{\mu_{\bullet}}:=\overline{{\rm Gr}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{\circ}}\to{\rm Gr}_{|{\mu_{\bullet}}|}=\cup_{\mu\leq|{\mu_{\bullet}}|}{\rm Gr}_{\mu}^{\circ}

given by
(g_{1},\dots,g_{d-1},g_{d}K)\mapsto g_{1}\cdots g_{d}K
, where
|{\mu_{\bullet}}|=\mu_{1}+\cdots+\mu_{d}\in Y^{+}
.

Theorem 1.5

(
[
41
]
,
[
45
]
,
[
21
, Theorem 1.3]
)
.

Let notations be as above. Let
\mu\in Y^{+}
with
\mu\leq|{\mu_{\bullet}}|
and
y\in{\rm Gr}_{\mu}^{\circ}
. Then

(1)
\dim m_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{-1}(y)\leqslant\langle\rho,|{\mu_{\bullet}}|-\mu\rangle
, and moreover, the number of irreducible components of
m_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{-1}(y)
having dimension
\langle\rho,|{\mu_{\bullet}}|-\mu\rangle
equals the multiplicity
m_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{\mu}
with which
\mathbb{B}_{\mu}^{\widehat{G}}
occurs in
\mathbb{B}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{\widehat{G}}:=\mathbb{B}_{\mu_{1}}^{\widehat{G}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbb{B}_{\mu_{d}}^{\widehat{G}}
.

(2)
m_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{-1}(y)
is equi-dimensional of dimension
\langle\rho,|{\mu_{\bullet}}|-\mu\rangle
if
{\mu_{\bullet}}
is minuscule.

Here
\rho=\rho_{G}
is the half sum of roots in
\Phi^{+}
.

Thanks to Zhu
[
58
, §3.1.3]
, there is a Cartesian square

\textstyle{X_{\mu_{\bullet}}({b_{\bullet}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm pr}}
\textstyle{G({\breve{F}})\times_{K}{\rm Gr}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{\circ}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\textrm{id}\times_{K}m_{\mu_{\bullet}}}
\textstyle{\cup_{\mu\leq|{\mu_{\bullet}}|}X_{\mu}(b)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G({\breve{F}})\times_{K}{\rm Gr}_{|{\mu_{\bullet}}|},}

where
{\rm pr}
is the projection to the first factor; the lower horizontal map is given by
g_{1}K\mapsto(g_{1},g_{1}^{-1}b\sigma(g_{1})K)
; the upper horizontal map is given by

(g_{1}K,\dots,g_{d}K)\mapsto(g_{1},g_{1}^{-1}g_{2},\dots,g_{d-1}^{-1}g_{d},g_{d}^{-1}b\sigma(g_{1})K).

Moreover, via the identification

\mathbb{J}_{b}\cong\mathbb{J}_{b_{\bullet}},\quad g\mapsto(g,\dots,g),

the above Cartesian square is
\mathbb{J}_{b}
-equivariant by left multiplication.

Corollary 1.6
.

Let the notation be as above. Then

{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu_{\bullet}}({b_{\bullet}})=\sqcup_{\mu\in Y^{+},m_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{\mu}\neq 0}\sqcup_{C\in{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu}(b)}{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}\overline{{\rm pr}^{-1}(C)}.

In particular,

\mathbb{J}_{b}\backslash{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu_{\bullet}}({b_{\bullet}})=\sqcup_{\mu\in Y^{+},m_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{\mu}\neq 0}\sqcup_{C\in\mathbb{J}_{b}\backslash{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu}(b)}{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}\overline{{\rm pr}^{-1}(C)}

and hence

|\mathbb{J}_{b}\backslash{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu_{\bullet}}({b_{\bullet}})|=\sum_{\mu\in Y^{+}}m_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{\mu}|\mathbb{J}_{b}\backslash{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu}(b)|.

As a consequence, if Theorem
0.5
is true, then the diagram of Theorem
0.7
is Cartesian if it is commutative.

Proof.

Using the same strategies of
[
9
]
and
[
11
]
we have

\dim X_{\mu_{\bullet}}({b_{\bullet}})=\langle\rho,|{\mu_{\bullet}}|-\nu_{b}\rangle-\frac{1}{2}{\rm def}(b)=\dim X_{\mu}(b)+\langle\rho,|{\mu_{\bullet}}|-\mu\rangle.

Let
\mu\in Y^{+}
and
C\in{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu}(b)
. By Theorem
1.5
(1),

\dim{\rm pr}^{-1}(C)=\dim C+\langle\rho,|{\mu_{\bullet}}|-\mu\rangle=\dim X_{\mu}(b)+\langle\rho,|{\mu_{\bullet}}|-\mu\rangle\leqslant\dim X_{\mu_{\bullet}}({b_{\bullet}}),

and moreover, the number of irreducible components of
{\rm pr}^{-1}(C)
having dimension
\dim X_{\mu_{\bullet}}({b_{\bullet}})
is equal to
m_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{\mu}
as desired.
∎

1.7.
Tensor structure

Let
\mu\in Y^{+}
. Recall that
V_{\mu}=V_{\mu}^{\widehat{G}}
denotes the simple
\widehat{G}
-module of highest weight
\mu
, and
\mathbb{B}_{\mu}=\mathbb{B}_{\mu}^{\widehat{G}}
denotes the crystal basis of
V_{\mu}
, which is a highest weight
\widehat{G}
-crystal. We refer to
[
30
]
,
[
38
]
and
[
56
, §3.3]
for the definition of
\widehat{G}
-crystals and a realization of
\mathbb{B}_{\mu}
using Littelmann’s path model.

For
{\lambda}\in Y
, let
\mathbb{B}_{\mu}({\lambda})
be the set of basis elements of weight
{\lambda}
. Then

|\mathbb{B}_{\mu}({\lambda})|=\dim V_{\mu}({\lambda}),

where
V_{\mu}({\lambda})
denotes the
{\lambda}
-weight space of
V_{\mu}
.

Recall that
{\rm MV}_{\mu}={\rm MV}_{\mu}^{\widehat{G}}
denotes the set of Mirković-Vilonen cycles in
{\rm Gr}_{\mu}
. By
[
1
, Theorem 3.1]
,
{\rm MV}_{\mu}
admits a
\widehat{G}
-crystal structure, which is isomorphic to
\mathbb{B}_{\mu}
. Let
S_{1}\in{\rm MV}_{\mu}({\lambda}_{1})
and
S_{2}\in{\rm MV}_{\mu}({\lambda}_{2})
be two Mirković-Vilonen cycles. The twisted product of
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
is

S_{1}\tilde{\times}S_{2}=(\theta_{{\lambda}_{1}}^{U})^{-1}(S_{1})t^{{\lambda}_{1}}\times_{K_{U}}S_{2}\subseteq G({\breve{F}})\times_{K}{\rm Gr},

where
\theta_{{\lambda}_{1}}^{U}:U({\breve{F}})\to{\rm Gr}
is given by
u\mapsto ut^{{\lambda}_{1}}K
. The convolution of
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
is defined by

S_{1}\star S_{2}=\overline{m(S_{1}\tilde{\times}S_{2})}\cap S^{{\lambda}_{1}+{\lambda}_{2}},

where
m:G({\breve{F}})\times_{K}{\rm Gr}\to{\rm Gr}
denotes the usual convolution map. Note that
\overline{S_{1}\star S_{2}}=\overline{m(S_{1}\tilde{\times}S_{2})}
.

Following
[
56
, Proposition 3.3.15]
, we fix from now on a bijection
\delta\mapsto S_{\delta}
from
\mathbb{B}_{\mu}
to
{\rm MV}_{\mu}
for
\mu\in Y^{+}
, which is compatible with the tensor product for
\widehat{G}
-crystals, that is,
S_{\delta_{1}\otimes\delta_{2}}=S_{\delta_{1}}\star S_{\delta_{2}}
.

1.8.
Admissible set

Let
P=MN
be a standard parabolic subgroup with standard Levi subgroup
M=\sigma(M)\supseteq T
and unipotent radical
N=\sigma(N)
. Let
\mathcal{E}
be one of groups
I
,
M({\breve{F}})
,
N({\breve{F}})
and
P({\breve{F}})
. For
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
set
\mathcal{E}_{n}=\mathcal{E}\cap K_{n}
, where
K_{n}=\{g\in K=G(\mathcal{O}_{{\breve{F}}});g\equiv 1\mod t^{n}\}
. Following
[
9
]
, we say a subset
\mathcal{D}\subseteq\mathcal{E}
is admissible if there exists some integer
r>0
such that
\mathcal{D}\mathcal{E}_{r}=\mathcal{D}
and
\mathcal{D}/\mathcal{E}_{r}\subseteq\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{E}_{r}
is a (bounded) locally closed subset. In this case, define

\dim\mathcal{D}=\dim\mathcal{D}/\mathcal{E}_{r}-\dim\mathcal{E}_{0}/\mathcal{E}_{r},

and moreover, we can define topological notions for
\mathcal{E}
, such as open/closed subsets, irreducible/connected components and so on, by passing to the quotient
\mathcal{D}/\mathcal{D}_{r}
. These definitions are independent of the choice of
r
since the natural quotient map
\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{E}_{n}\to\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{E}_{n+1}
is an affine space fiber bundle. For instance, the irreducible components of
\mathcal{D}
is defined to be the inverse images of the irreducible components of
\mathcal{D}/\mathcal{E}_{r}\subseteq\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{E}_{r}
under the natural projection
\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{E}_{r}
. We denote by
{\rm Irr}\mathcal{D}
the set of irreducible components of
\mathcal{D}
in this sense.

2.
The set
X_{\mu}^{P,{\lambda}}(b)

Keep the notations in the introduction and §
1
. In this section, we introduce a decomposition
X_{\mu}(b)=\sqcup_{{\lambda}\in Y}X_{\mu}^{P,{\lambda}}(b)
with respect to certain parabolic subgroup
P\subseteq G
, and study the irreducible components of
X_{\mu}^{P,{\lambda}}(b)
.

2.1.
The set
H^{P}(C)

Let
P=MN
be a standard parabolic subgroup as in §
1.8
. Suppose
b\in M({\breve{F}})
such that
b
is basic in
M({\breve{F}})
and
\nu_{M}(b)=\nu_{G}(b)
. Moreover, we always assume that
b\in N_{T}({\breve{F}})
is a lift of an element in
\Omega_{M}
, whose image in
\tilde{W}_{M}
is still denote by
b
. Notice that
b
normalizes
N({\breve{F}})I_{M}
. Let
\phi_{b}^{P}:N({\breve{F}})I_{M}\to N({\breve{F}})I_{M}
be the Lang’s map given by
h\mapsto h^{-1}b\sigma(h)b^{-1}
. For
\upsilon\in Y
let
\theta_{\lambda}^{P}:N({\breve{F}})I_{M}\to{\rm Gr}
be the map given by
h\mapsto ht^{\lambda}K
.

Let
C\subseteq{\rm Gr}
be locally closed and irreducible. We define

H^{P}(C)=H^{P}(C;b)=\phi_{b}^{P}((\theta_{\lambda}^{P})^{-1}(C))\subseteq N({\breve{F}})I_{M},

where
{\lambda}\in Y
such that
N({\breve{F}})I_{M}t^{\lambda}K/K\cap C
is open dense in
C
. In this case, the map
\gamma^{G}
of Theorem
0.5
can be formulated by

\{(ht^{\lambda})^{-1}b\sigma(ht^{\lambda})K;h\in(\theta_{\lambda}^{P})^{-1}(C)\}=t^{-{\lambda}}H^{P}(C)t^{b\sigma({\lambda})}K/K,

where
b\sigma({\lambda})\in Y
is defined by the affine action of
\tilde{W}\rtimes\langle\sigma\rangle
on
Y
, see §
1.1
.

For
\mu\in Y^{+}
and
{\lambda}\in Y
we set
X_{\mu}^{{\lambda},P}(b)=N({\breve{F}})I_{M}t^{\lambda}K/K\cap X_{\mu}(b)
.

Lemma 2.1
.

The map
C\mapsto H^{P}(C)
for
C\in{\rm Irr}X_{\mu}^{{\lambda},P}(b)
induces a bijection

(N({\breve{F}})I_{M}\cap\mathbb{J}_{b})\backslash{\rm Irr}X_{\mu}^{{\lambda},P}(b)\cong{\rm Irr}(t^{\lambda}Kt^{\mu}Kt^{-b\sigma({\lambda})}\cap N({\breve{F}})I_{M}).

In particular,
H^{P}(C;b)
is invariant under left/right multiplication by
K_{T}
.

Proof.

Note that
bt^{\chi}K=t^{b(\chi)}K
and
Kt^{-\chi}b^{-1}=Kt^{-b(\chi)}
for
\chi\in Y
. Therefore,

\displaystyle(\theta_{\lambda}^{P})^{-1}(X_{\mu}^{{\lambda},P}(b))

\displaystyle=(\phi_{b}^{P})^{-1}(t^{\lambda}Kt^{\mu}Kt^{-\sigma({\lambda})}b^{-1}\cap N({\breve{F}})I_{M})

\displaystyle=(\phi_{b}^{P})^{-1}(t^{\lambda}Kt^{\mu}Kt^{-b\sigma({\lambda})}\cap N({\breve{F}})I_{M}),

As
\phi_{b}^{P}
is an etale covering of
N({\breve{F}})I_{M}
with Galois group
N({\breve{F}})I_{M}\cap\mathbb{J}_{b}
, the map
C\mapsto H^{P}(C)
for
C\in{\rm Irr}X_{\mu}^{{\lambda},P}(b)
induces a bijection

(N({\breve{F}})I_{M}\cap\mathbb{J}_{b})\backslash{\rm Irr}X_{\mu}^{{\lambda},P}(b)\cong{\rm Irr}(t^{\lambda}Kt^{\mu}Kt^{-b\sigma({\lambda})}\cap N({\breve{F}})I_{M}).

The proof is finished.
∎

Now we focus on the basic case. Let
I_{\rm der}
denote the derived subgroup of
I
.

Corollary 2.2
.

Assume
b
is basic. Then the map
C\mapsto H_{\rm der}^{G}(C)
for
C\in{\rm Irr}X_{\mu}^{{\lambda},G}(b)
induces a bijection

(I_{\rm der}\cap\mathbb{J}_{b})\backslash{\rm Irr}X_{\mu}^{{\lambda},G}(b)\cong{\rm Irr}(t^{\lambda}Kt^{\mu}Kt^{-b\sigma({\lambda})}\cap I_{\rm der}).

Here
H^{G}_{\rm der}(C)=\phi_{b}^{G}((\theta_{\lambda}^{G})^{-1}(C)\cap I_{\rm der})
.

Proof.

Note that
It^{\lambda}K/K=I_{\rm der}t^{\lambda}K/K
and that
\phi_{b}^{G}
restricts to an etale covering of
I_{\rm der}
with Galois group
I_{\rm der}\cap\mathbb{J}_{b}
. Then the statement follows in the same way of Lemma
2.1
.
∎

recall that
p:\tilde{W}\rtimes\langle\sigma\rangle\to W_{0}\rtimes\langle\sigma\rangle
is the natural projection.

Lemma 2.3
.

Assume
b
is basic. For
{\lambda}\in Y
and
C\in{\rm Irr}X_{\mu}^{{\lambda},G}(b)
we have

t^{-{\lambda}^{\prime}}H^{G}(C^{\prime};b^{\prime})t^{b^{\prime}\sigma({\lambda}^{\prime})}=p(\omega)t^{-{\lambda}}H^{G}(C;b)t^{b\sigma({\lambda})}p(\omega)^{-1},

where
\omega\in\Omega
,
\dot{\omega}\in N_{T}({\breve{F}})
is a lift of
\omega
,
{\lambda}^{\prime}=\omega({\lambda})
,
C^{\prime}=\dot{\omega}C
and
b^{\prime}=\dot{\omega}b\sigma(\dot{\omega})^{-1}
. Note that the right hand side is independent of the choice of
\dot{\omega}
by Lemma
2.1
.

Proof.

As
\omega\in\Omega
, we have
\dot{\omega}I\dot{\omega}^{-1}=I
and hence

C^{\prime}=\dot{\omega}C\subseteq\dot{\omega}It^{\lambda}K/K=It^{\omega({\lambda})}K/K.

So
(\theta_{{\lambda}^{\prime}}^{G})^{-1}(C^{\prime})=\dot{\omega}(\theta_{\lambda}^{G})^{-1}(C)\dot{\omega}^{-1}
, and the statement follows by definition.
∎

Corollary 2.4
.

In the superbasic case, the map
\gamma^{G}
in Theorem
0.5
is independent of the choice of
b
.

Proof.

Suppose
b
is superbasic and let
b,b^{\prime},{\lambda},{\lambda}^{\prime},\omega,C,C^{\prime}
be as in Lemma
2.3
. Let
\gamma_{b}^{G}
(resp.
\gamma_{b^{\prime}}^{G}
) be the map
\gamma^{G}
in Theorem
0.5
defined with respect to
b
(resp.
b^{\prime}
). We need to show that
\gamma^{G}_{b}(C)=\gamma^{G}_{b^{\prime}}(C^{\prime})
. By definition (for the superbasic case), it suffices to show that

(\epsilon_{\lambda}^{G})^{-1}t^{-{\lambda}}H^{G}(C;b)t^{b\sigma({\lambda})}K/K=(\epsilon_{{\lambda}^{\prime}}^{G})^{-1}t^{-{\lambda}^{\prime}}H^{G}(C^{\prime};b^{\prime})t^{b^{\prime}\sigma({\lambda}^{\prime})}K/K,

which follows from Lemma
1.3
(3) and Lemma
2.3
.
∎

Corollary 2.5
.

Assume
b
is basic. For
{\lambda}\in Y
there are natural bijections

(I\cap\mathbb{J}_{b})\backslash{\rm Irr}X_{\mu}^{{\lambda},G}(b)\cong{\rm Irr}(t^{\lambda}Kt^{\mu}Kt^{-b\sigma({\lambda})}\cap I_{U_{\lambda}})\cong(I_{\rm der}\cap\mathbb{J}_{b})\backslash{\rm Irr}X_{\mu}^{{\lambda},G}(b),

where
I_{U_{\lambda}}=I\cap U_{\lambda}
and
U_{\lambda}
is as in §
1.5
. Moreover, for
C\in{\rm Irr}X_{\mu}^{{\lambda},G}(b)
we have

H^{G}(C)=K_{T}H^{G}_{\rm der}(C)=H^{G}_{\rm der}(C)K_{T}.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.1
the map
C\mapsto H^{G}(C)
for
C\in{\rm Irr}X_{\mu}^{{\lambda},G}(b)
induces a bijection

(a)

\displaystyle(I\cap\mathbb{J}_{b})\backslash{\rm Irr}X_{\mu}^{{\lambda},G}(b)\cong{\rm Irr}(t^{\lambda}Kt^{\mu}Kt^{-b\sigma({\lambda})}\cap I)\cong{\rm Irr}(t^{\lambda}Kt^{\mu}Kt^{-b\sigma({\lambda})}\cap I_{U_{\lambda}}),

where the second bijection follows from that

t^{\lambda}Kt^{\mu}Kt^{-b\sigma({\lambda})}\cap I=I_{\lambda}^{-}(t^{\lambda}Kt^{\mu}Kt^{-b\sigma({\lambda})}\cap I_{U_{\lambda}}).

Similarly, by Corollary
2.2
we have

(b)

\displaystyle(I_{\rm der}\cap\mathbb{J}_{b})\backslash{\rm Irr}X_{\mu}^{{\lambda},G}(b)\cong{\rm Irr}(t^{\lambda}Kt^{\mu}Kt^{-b\sigma({\lambda})}\cap I_{\rm der})\cong{\rm Irr}(t^{\lambda}Kt^{\mu}Kt^{-b\sigma({\lambda})}\cap I_{U_{\lambda}}).

So the first statement follows.

By (a) and (b), there exist
\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}\in{\rm Irr}(t^{\lambda}Kt^{\mu}Kt^{-b\sigma({\lambda})}\cap I_{U_{\lambda}})
such that

(I_{\lambda}^{-}\cap I_{\rm der})\mathcal{Z}=H_{\rm der}^{G}(C)\subseteq H^{G}(C)=I_{\lambda}^{-}\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}.

In particular,
\mathcal{Z}=\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}
. Moreover,
K_{T}
normalises
t^{\lambda}Kt^{\mu}Kt^{-b\sigma({\lambda})}\cap I_{U_{\lambda}}
, and hence normalises each of its irreducible components. So we have

H^{G}(C)=I_{\lambda}^{-}\mathcal{Z}=(I_{\lambda}^{-}\cap I_{\rm der})K_{T}\mathcal{Z}=H_{\rm der}^{G}(C)K_{T}=K_{T}H_{\rm der}^{G}(C).

The second statement is proved.
∎

Lemma 2.6
.

For
{\lambda},\chi\in Y
there is a natural bijection

{\rm Irr}(t^{\lambda}Kt^{\mu}Kt^{-\chi}\cap I_{U_{\lambda}})\cong{\rm Irr}(Kt^{-\mu}K/K\cap t^{-\chi}I_{U_{\lambda}}t^{\lambda}K/K).

Proof.

The map
g\mapsto t^{-\chi}g^{-1}t^{\lambda}
gives a bijection

t^{\lambda}Kt^{\mu}Kt^{-\chi}\cap I_{U_{\lambda}}\cong Kt^{-\mu}K\cap t^{-\chi}I_{U_{\lambda}}t^{\lambda}.

By the definition of
U_{\lambda}
we have
K_{U_{\lambda}}\subseteq t^{-{\lambda}}I_{U_{\lambda}}t^{\lambda}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle{\rm Irr}(t^{\lambda}Kt^{\mu}Kt^{-\chi}\cap I_{U_{\lambda}})

\displaystyle\cong{\rm Irr}((Kt^{-\mu}K\cap t^{-\chi}I_{U_{\lambda}}t^{\lambda})/K_{U_{\lambda}})

\displaystyle={\rm Irr}(Kt^{-\mu}K/K\cap t^{-\chi}I_{U_{\lambda}}t^{\lambda}K/K),

where the identity follows from that
t^{-\chi}I_{U_{\lambda}}t^{\lambda}/K_{U_{\lambda}}\cong t^{-\chi}I_{U_{\lambda}}t^{\lambda}K/K
.
∎

2.2.
The minuscule and basic case

Suppose
\mu\in Y^{+}
is minuscule and
b
is basic. For
D\subseteq\tilde{W}
we set
D\cap\mathbb{J}_{b}=\{{\tilde{w}}\in D;b\sigma({\tilde{w}})b^{-1}={\tilde{w}}\}
.

For
{\lambda}\in Y
we write
X_{\mu}^{\lambda}(b)=X_{\mu}^{{\lambda},G}(b)=It^{\lambda}K/K\cap X_{\mu}(b)
. Let
\mathcal{A}_{\mu,b}^{G}
and
\mathcal{A}_{\mu,b}^{G,{\rm top}}
be the sets of
{\lambda}\in Y
such that
X_{\mu}^{\lambda}(b)\neq\emptyset
and
\dim X_{\mu}^{\lambda}(b)=\dim X_{\mu}(b)
respectively.

For
\alpha\in\Phi
define
\alpha^{i}=p(b\sigma)^{i}(\alpha)\in\Phi
and
{\tilde{\alpha}}^{i}=(b\sigma)^{i}({\tilde{\alpha}})\in\tilde{\Phi}
for
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, where
{\tilde{\alpha}}
is as in §
1.2
and
p:\tilde{W}\rtimes\langle\sigma\rangle\to W_{0}\rtimes\langle\sigma\rangle
is the natural projection.

For
{\lambda}\in\mathcal{A}_{\mu,b}^{G}
define
{\lambda}^{\natural}=-{\lambda}+b\sigma({\lambda})
, and denote by
R_{\mu,b}^{G}({\lambda})
the set of roots
\alpha\in\Phi
such that
\langle\alpha,{\lambda}^{\natural}\rangle=-1
and
{\lambda}_{\alpha}\geqslant 1
. By Lemma
2.7
(1) below, this condition is equivalent to that
\langle\alpha,{\lambda}^{\natural}\rangle=-1
and
{\lambda}_{\alpha^{-1}}\geqslant 0
.

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
{\lambda}\in Y
. Then we have (1)
\langle\alpha,{\lambda}^{\natural}\rangle={\lambda}_{\alpha^{-1}}-{\lambda}_{\alpha}
for
\alpha\in\Phi
and (2)
{\tilde{w}}({\lambda})^{\natural}=p({\tilde{w}})({\lambda}^{\natural})
for
{\tilde{w}}\in\tilde{W}\cap\mathbb{J}_{b}
.

Proof.

Suppose
b\in t^{\tau}W_{0}
for some
\tau\in Y
. Then

\langle\alpha,{\lambda}^{\natural}\rangle=-\langle\alpha,{\lambda}\rangle+\langle\alpha,\tau\rangle+\langle\alpha^{-1},{\lambda}\rangle.

As
b\in\Omega
,
b\sigma
preserves the fundamental alcove
\Delta
and hence preserves the set
\{\tilde{\beta};\beta\in\Phi\}
, see §
1.2
. Thus
\alpha,\alpha^{-1}
are both positive or negative if
\langle\alpha,\tau\rangle=0
;
\alpha<0
and
\alpha^{-1}>0
if
\langle\alpha,\tau\rangle=-1
;
\alpha>0
and
\alpha^{-1}<0
if
\langle\alpha,\tau\rangle=1
. In all cases we have
\langle\alpha,{\lambda}^{\natural}\rangle={\lambda}_{\alpha^{-1}}-{\lambda}_{\alpha}
as desired. For
{\tilde{w}}\in\tilde{W}\cap\mathbb{J}_{b}
, it follows that

{\tilde{w}}({\lambda})^{\natural}=-{\tilde{w}}({\lambda})+b\sigma{\tilde{w}}({\lambda})=-{\tilde{w}}({\lambda})+{\tilde{w}}b\sigma({\lambda})=p({\tilde{w}})(-{\lambda}+b\sigma({\lambda}))=p({\tilde{w}})({\lambda}^{\natural}).

The proof is finished.
∎

Lemma 2.8
.

We have
R_{\mu,b}^{G}(\omega({\lambda}))=p(\omega)R_{\mu,b}^{G}({\lambda})
for
\omega\in\Omega\cap\mathbb{J}_{b}
,
{\lambda}\in\mathcal{A}_{\mu,b}^{G}
.

Proof.

Notice that
p({\tilde{w}})(\gamma)^{i}=p({\tilde{w}})(\gamma^{i})
for
\gamma\in\Phi
,
{\tilde{w}}\in\tilde{W}\cap\mathbb{J}_{b}
and
i\in\mathbb{Z}
. The statement now follows from Lemma
1.3
(3) and Lemma
2.7
(1).
∎

Proposition 2.9
.

Suppose
\mu
is minuscule. Then
{\lambda}\in\mathcal{A}_{\mu,b}^{G}
, that is
X_{\mu}^{\lambda}(b)\neq\emptyset
, if and only if
{\lambda}^{\natural}
is conjugate to
\mu
by
W_{0}
. Moreover, in this case,

(1)
t^{\lambda}Kt^{\mu}Kt^{-b\sigma({\lambda})}\cap I=I_{\lambda}\prod_{\delta\in R_{\mu,b}^{G}({\lambda})}U_{\delta}(t^{\langle\delta,{\lambda}\rangle-1}\mathcal{O}_{{\breve{F}}})
;

(2)
X_{\mu}^{\lambda}(b)
is smooth and
I\cap\mathbb{J}_{b}
acts on
{\rm Irr}X_{\mu}^{\lambda}(b)
transitively;

(3)
\dim X_{\mu}^{\lambda}(b)=|R_{\mu,b}^{G}({\lambda})|
.

Proof.

By Corollary
2.5
we have

(I\cap\mathbb{J}_{b})\backslash{\rm Irr}X_{\mu}^{\lambda}(b)\cong{\rm Irr}(t^{\lambda}Kt^{\mu}Kt^{-b\sigma({\lambda})}\cap I)\cong{\rm Irr}(Kt^{\mu}K\cap t^{-{\lambda}}I_{U_{\lambda}}t^{{\lambda}}t^{{\lambda}^{\natural}}).

As
\mu
is minuscule, we see that
\emptyset\neq Kt^{\mu}K\cap t^{-{\lambda}}I_{U_{\lambda}}t^{{\lambda}}t^{{\lambda}^{\natural}}\subseteq U_{\lambda}({\breve{F}})t^{{\lambda}^{\natural}}
, that is,
{\lambda}\in\mathcal{A}_{\mu,b}^{G}
, if and only if
{\lambda}^{\natural}
is conjugate to
\mu
. Moreover, in this case,

Kt^{\mu}K\cap U_{\lambda}({\breve{F}})t^{{\lambda}^{\natural}}=K_{U_{\lambda}}t^{{\lambda}^{\natural}}K_{U_{\lambda}}=(\prod_{\alpha}U_{\alpha}(\mathcal{O}_{\breve{F}})\prod_{\beta}U_{\beta}(t^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{\breve{F}}))t^{{\lambda}^{\natural}},

where
\alpha,\beta
range over the roots of
U_{\lambda}
such that
\langle\alpha,{\lambda}^{\natural}\rangle\geqslant 0
and
\langle\beta,{\lambda}^{\natural}\rangle=-1
in any fixed orders. Here the root subgroups
U_{\beta}
commute with each other since
{\lambda}^{\natural}
is minuscule. On the other hand,
t^{-{\lambda}}I_{U_{\lambda}}t^{{\lambda}}t^{{\lambda}^{\natural}}=(\prod_{\gamma}U_{\gamma}(t^{-{\lambda}_{\gamma}}\mathcal{O}_{\breve{F}}))t^{{\lambda}^{\natural}}
, where
\gamma
ranges over the roots of
U_{\lambda}
(or
{\lambda}_{\gamma}\geqslant 0
) in the above fixed order. Thus

K_{U_{\lambda}}t^{{\lambda}^{\natural}}K_{U_{\lambda}}\cap t^{-{\lambda}}I_{U_{\lambda}}t^{{\lambda}}t^{{\lambda}^{\natural}}=(K_{U_{\lambda}}\prod_{\delta}U_{\delta}(t^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{\breve{F}}))t^{{\lambda}^{\natural}},

where
\delta
ranges over
R_{\mu,b}^{G}({\lambda})=\{\gamma\in\Phi;\langle\gamma,{\lambda}^{\natural}\rangle=-1,{\lambda}_{\gamma}\geqslant 1\}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle t^{\lambda}Kt^{\mu}Kt^{-b\sigma({\lambda})}\cap I

\displaystyle=I_{\lambda}t^{\lambda}(Kt^{\mu}K\cap t^{-{\lambda}}I_{U_{\lambda}}t^{{\lambda}}t^{{\lambda}^{\natural}})t^{-b\sigma({\lambda})}

\displaystyle=I_{\lambda}t^{\lambda}(K_{U_{\lambda}}t^{{\lambda}^{\natural}}K_{U_{\lambda}}\cap t^{-{\lambda}}I_{U_{\lambda}}t^{{\lambda}}t^{{\lambda}^{\natural}})t^{-b\sigma({\lambda})}

\displaystyle=I_{\lambda}t^{\lambda}(K_{U_{\lambda}}\prod_{\delta\in R_{\mu,b}^{G}({\lambda})}U_{\delta}(t^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{\breve{F}}))t^{-{\lambda}}

\displaystyle=I_{\lambda}\prod_{\delta\in R_{\mu,b}^{G}({\lambda})}U_{\delta}(t^{\langle\delta,{\lambda}\rangle-1}\mathcal{O}_{{\breve{F}}}).

So the statement (1) follows. The statement (2) follows from Corollary
2.5
by noticing that
t^{\lambda}Kt^{\mu}Kt^{-b\sigma({\lambda})}\cap I
is smooth and irreducible.

As
(\theta_{\lambda}^{G})^{-1}(X_{\mu}^{\lambda}(b))=(\phi_{b}^{G})^{-1}(t^{\lambda}Kt^{\mu}Kt^{-b\sigma({\lambda})}\cap I)
, we deduce by (1) that

\displaystyle\dim X_{\mu}^{\lambda}(b)

\displaystyle=\dim((\theta_{\lambda}^{G})^{-1}(X_{\mu}^{\lambda}(b))/I_{\lambda})

\displaystyle=\dim((\theta_{\lambda}^{G})^{-1}(X_{\mu}^{\lambda}(b)))-\dim I_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\dim(t^{\lambda}Kt^{\mu}Kt^{-b\sigma({\lambda})}\cap I)-\dim I_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=|R_{\mu,b}^{G}({\lambda})|.

So the statement (3) follows.
∎

2.3.
The set
H^{P^{d}}(C)

Let
P=MN
and
b\in M(L)
be as in §
2.1
. Let
G^{d}
,
\sigma_{\bullet}
,
{b_{\bullet}}
,
{\mu_{\bullet}}
and
{\rm pr}
be as in §
1.6
. We also denote by
{\rm pr}
the projections
G^{d}({\breve{F}})\to G({\breve{F}})
and
Y^{d}\to Y
to the first factors.

Let
C\in{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu_{\bullet}}({b_{\bullet}})
and
{\lambda_{\bullet}}\in Y^{d}
such that
(N({\breve{F}})I_{M})^{d}t^{\lambda_{\bullet}}K^{d}/K^{d}\cap C
is open dense in
C
. By Corollary
1.6
,
\overline{{\rm pr}(C)}\in{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu}(b)
for some
\mu\in Y^{+}
. Let
{\lambda}={\rm pr}({\lambda_{\bullet}})
. Then
N({\breve{F}})I_{M}t^{\lambda}K/K\cap{\rm pr}(C)
is open dense in
\overline{{\rm pr}(C)}
. Set
{\lambda}^{\dagger}_{\bullet}={b_{\bullet}}\sigma_{\bullet}({\lambda_{\bullet}})
,
\phi_{b_{\bullet}}=\phi_{b_{\bullet}}^{P^{d}}
and
\theta_{\lambda_{\bullet}}=\theta_{\lambda}^{P^{d}}
. By Lemma
2.1
,

H^{P^{d}}(C)=\phi_{b_{\bullet}}(\theta_{\lambda_{\bullet}}^{-1}(C))\in{\rm Irr}(t^{\lambda_{\bullet}}K^{d}t^{\mu_{\bullet}}K^{d}t^{-{\lambda}^{\dagger}_{\bullet}}\cap(N({\breve{F}})I_{M})^{d}).

So we can write

H^{P^{d}}(C)=H_{1}(C)\times\cdots\times H_{d}(C),

where
H_{\tau}(C)\in{\rm Irr}(t^{{\lambda}_{\tau}}Kt^{\mu_{\tau}}Kt^{-{\lambda}^{\dagger}_{\tau}}\cap N({\breve{F}})I_{M})
for
1\leqslant\tau\leqslant d
with
{\mu_{\bullet}}=(\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{d})
,
{\lambda_{\bullet}}=({\lambda}_{1},\dots,{\lambda}_{d})
and
{\lambda}^{\dagger}_{\bullet}=({\lambda}_{1}^{\dagger},\dots,{\lambda}_{d}^{\dagger})
.

Lemma 2.10
.

Let notations be as above. Then we have

\overline{H^{P}(\overline{{\rm pr}(C)})}=\overline{H_{1}(C)\cdots H_{d}(C)}\subseteq N({\breve{F}})I_{M}

As a consequence,

\overline{t^{-{\lambda}}H^{P}(\overline{{\rm pr}(C)})t^{b\sigma({\lambda})}K/K}=\overline{t^{-{\lambda}_{1}}H_{1}(C)t^{{\lambda}^{\dagger}_{1}}\cdots t^{-{\lambda}_{d}}H_{d}(C)t^{{\lambda}^{\dagger}_{d}}K/K}.

Proof.

As
\overline{{\rm pr}((N({\breve{F}})I_{M})^{d}t^{\lambda_{\bullet}}K^{d}/K^{d}\cap C)}=\overline{{\rm pr}(C)}
, we see that

\overline{{\rm pr}(\theta_{\lambda_{\bullet}}^{-1}(C))}=\overline{(\theta_{\lambda}^{P})^{-1}(\overline{{\rm pr}(C)})}\subseteq N({\breve{F}})I_{M}.

On the other hand, the equality
H^{P^{d}}(C)=\phi_{b_{\bullet}}(\theta_{{\lambda_{\bullet}}}^{-1}(C))
means that

H_{1}(C)\times\cdots\times H_{d}(C)=\{(h_{1}^{-1}h_{2},\dots,h_{d-1}^{-1}h_{d},h_{d}^{-1}b\sigma(h_{1})b^{-1});(h_{1},\dots,h_{d})\in\theta_{\lambda_{\bullet}}^{-1}(C)\}.

In particular,

H_{1}(C)\cdots H_{d}(C)=\phi_{b}^{P}({\rm pr}(\theta_{\lambda_{\bullet}}^{-1}(C))).

So
\overline{H^{P}(\overline{{\rm pr}(C)})}=\overline{\phi_{b}^{P}((\theta_{\lambda}^{P})^{-1}(\overline{{\rm pr}(C)}))}=\overline{\phi_{b}^{P}({\rm pr}(\theta_{\lambda_{\bullet}}^{-1}(C)))}=\overline{H_{1}(C)\cdots H_{d}(C)}
as desired.
∎

3.
The superbasic case

In this section we consider the case where
b
is superbasic.

3.1.
The formulation

For
d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 1}
let
\sigma_{\bullet}
,
{b_{\bullet}}
,
{\mu_{\bullet}}
and
P=G
be as in §
1.6
. Let
C\in{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu_{\bullet}}({b_{\bullet}})
and let
{\lambda_{\bullet}}\in Y^{d}
such that
(I^{d}t^{{\lambda_{\bullet}}}K^{d}/K^{d})\cap C
is open dense in
C
. Following §
2.3
let

H^{G^{d}}(C)=H_{1}(C)\times\cdots\times H_{d}(C)\in{\rm Irr}(t^{\lambda_{\bullet}}K^{d}t^{\mu_{\bullet}}K^{d}t^{-{b_{\bullet}}\sigma_{\bullet}({\lambda_{\bullet}})}\cap I^{d}),

where
H_{\tau}(C)\in{\rm Irr}(t^{{\lambda}_{\tau}}Kt^{\mu_{\tau}}Kt^{-{\lambda}_{\tau}^{\dagger}}\cap I)
for
1\leqslant\tau\leqslant d
with
{\lambda_{\bullet}}=({\lambda}_{1},\dots,{\lambda}_{d})
and
{\lambda}_{\bullet}^{\dagger}={b_{\bullet}}\sigma_{\bullet}({\lambda_{\bullet}})=({\lambda}_{1}^{\dagger},\dots,{\lambda}_{d}^{\dagger})
. We set
X_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{{\lambda_{\bullet}}}({b_{\bullet}})=X_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{{\lambda_{\bullet}},G^{d}}({b_{\bullet}})
for simplicity.

The main result of this section is

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
C,{\lambda_{\bullet}},{\lambda}_{\bullet}^{\dagger}
be as above. There is
\gamma^{G^{d}}(C)=(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{d})\in\mathbb{B}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{\widehat{G}^{d}}
such that

\overline{t^{-{\lambda}_{1}}H_{1}(C)t^{{\lambda}_{1}^{\dagger}}\times_{K}\cdots\times_{K}t^{-{\lambda}_{d}}H_{d}(C)t^{{\lambda}_{d}^{\dagger}}K/K}=\epsilon_{{\lambda}_{1}}\overline{S_{\gamma_{1}}\tilde{\times}\cdots\tilde{\times}S_{\gamma_{d}}},

where
\epsilon_{{\lambda}_{1}}=\epsilon_{{\lambda}_{1}}^{G}
is as in §
1.5
. Moreover, the map
C\mapsto\gamma^{G^{d}}(C)
factors through a bijection

\mathbb{J}_{b_{\bullet}}\backslash{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu_{\bullet}}({b_{\bullet}})\cong\mathbb{B}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{\widehat{G}^{d}}({\underline{\lambda}}_{G^{d}}({b_{\bullet}})).

In particular, Theorem
0.5
is true if
b
is superbasic by taking
d=1
.

3.2.
Reduction procedure

First we show how to pass to the case where
G={\rm Res}_{E/F}{\rm GL}_{n}
with
E/F
an unramified extension.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
f:G\to G^{\prime}
be a central isogeny. Then Theorem
3.1
is true for
G
if and only if it is true for
G^{\prime}
.

Proof.

We still denote by
f
the induced maps
G({\breve{F}})\to G^{\prime}({\breve{F}})
,
{\rm Gr}_{G}\to{\rm Gr}_{G^{\prime}}
and so on. Let
\sigma^{\prime}
be the Frobenius automorphism of
G^{\prime}
. Let
C\in{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu_{\bullet}}({b_{\bullet}})
and
{\lambda_{\bullet}}\in Y^{d}
such that
I^{d}t^{\lambda_{\bullet}}K^{d}/K^{d}\cap C
is open dense in
C
. Let
{\omega_{\bullet}}\in\pi_{1}(G^{d})
such that the corresponding connected component
{\rm Gr}_{G^{d}}^{\omega_{\bullet}}
contains
C
. Let
K^{\prime}=G^{\prime}(\mathcal{O}_{\breve{F}})
and
I^{\prime}\subseteq K^{\prime}
the Iwahori subgroup containing
f(I)
. Denote by
\mu_{\bullet}^{\prime},{\lambda}_{\bullet}^{\prime},C^{\prime},b_{\bullet}^{\prime},\omega_{\bullet}^{\prime}
the images of
{\mu_{\bullet}},{\lambda_{\bullet}},C,{b_{\bullet}},{\omega_{\bullet}}
under
f
respectively. Write
{\lambda_{\bullet}}=({\lambda}_{1},\dots,{\lambda}_{d})
,
{b_{\bullet}}=(b_{1},\dots,b_{d})
,
{\lambda}_{\bullet}^{\prime}=({\lambda}_{1}^{\prime},\dots,{\lambda}_{d}^{\prime})
and
b_{\bullet}^{\prime}=(b_{1}^{\prime},\dots,b_{d}^{\prime})
.

By
[
3
, Corollary 2.4.2]
and
[
18
, Proposition 3.1]
,
f
induces a homeomorphism

X_{\mu_{\bullet}}({b_{\bullet}})\cap{\rm Gr}_{G^{d}}^{\omega_{\bullet}}=:X_{\mu_{\bullet}}({b_{\bullet}})^{\omega_{\bullet}}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}X_{\mu_{\bullet}^{\prime}}(b_{\bullet}^{\prime})^{\omega_{\bullet}^{\prime}}:=X_{\mu_{\bullet}^{\prime}}(b_{\bullet}^{\prime})^{\omega_{\bullet}^{\prime}}\cap{\rm Gr}_{{G^{\prime}}^{d}}^{\omega_{\bullet}^{\prime}}.

So
C^{\prime}\in{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu_{\bullet}^{\prime}}(b_{\bullet}^{\prime})
and
{I^{\prime}}^{d}t^{{\lambda}_{\bullet}^{\prime}}{K^{\prime}}^{d}/{K^{\prime}}^{d}\cap C^{\prime}
is open dense in
C^{\prime}
. Moreover, as
f(I_{\rm der})=I_{\rm der}^{\prime}
we have

\displaystyle f((\theta_{\lambda_{\bullet}}^{G^{d}})^{-1}(C)\cap(I_{\rm der})^{d})

\displaystyle=(\theta_{{\lambda}_{\bullet}^{\prime}}^{{G^{\prime}}^{d}})^{-1}(C^{\prime})\cap(I_{\rm der}^{\prime})^{d}

\displaystyle f(H_{\rm der}^{G^{d}}(C))

\displaystyle=H_{\rm der}^{{G^{\prime}}^{d}}(C^{\prime}),

where
H_{\rm der}^{G^{d}}(C)
and
H_{\rm der}^{{G^{\prime}}^{d}}(C^{\prime})
are defined as in Corollary
2.2
. By Corollary
2.3
,

\displaystyle H^{G^{d}}(C)

\displaystyle=H_{\rm der}^{G^{d}}(C)T^{d}(\mathcal{O}_{\breve{F}})=T^{d}(\mathcal{O}_{\breve{F}})H_{\rm der}^{G^{d}}(C);

\displaystyle H^{{G^{\prime}}^{d}}(C^{\prime})

\displaystyle=H_{\rm der}^{{G^{\prime}}^{d}}(C^{\prime}){T^{\prime}}^{d}(\mathcal{O}_{\breve{F}})={T^{\prime}}^{d}(\mathcal{O}_{\breve{F}})H_{\rm der}^{{G^{\prime}}^{d}}(C^{\prime}).

Therefore,
f
induces a surjection and hence a bijection

\displaystyle\quad\ t^{-{\lambda}_{a}}H_{a}(C)t^{{\lambda}_{a}^{\dagger}}\times_{K}\cdots\times_{K}t^{-{\lambda}_{c}}H_{c}(C)t^{{\lambda}_{c}^{\dagger}}K/K

\displaystyle=t^{-{\lambda}_{a}}H_{{\rm der},a}(C)t^{{\lambda}_{a}^{\dagger}}\times_{K}\cdots\times_{K}t^{-{\lambda}_{c}}H_{{\rm der},c}(C)t^{{\lambda}_{c}^{\dagger}}K/K

\displaystyle\cong t^{-{\lambda}_{a}^{\prime}}H_{{\rm der},a}(C^{\prime})t^{{\lambda}_{a}^{\prime{\dagger}}}\times_{K^{\prime}}\cdots\times_{K^{\prime}}t^{-{\lambda}_{c}^{\prime}}H_{{\rm der},c}(C^{\prime})t^{{\lambda}_{c}^{\prime{\dagger}}}K^{\prime}/K^{\prime}

\displaystyle=t^{-{\lambda}_{a}^{\prime}}(H_{a}(C^{\prime})t^{{\lambda}_{a}^{\prime{\dagger}}}\times_{K^{\prime}}\cdots\times_{K^{\prime}}t^{-{\lambda}_{c}^{\prime}}H_{c}(C^{\prime})t^{{\lambda}_{c}^{\prime{\dagger}}}K^{\prime}/K^{\prime},

where, as in §
2.3
we write

\displaystyle H_{\rm der}^{G^{d}}(C)

\displaystyle=H_{{\rm der},1}(C)\times\cdots\times H_{{\rm der},d}(C)

\displaystyle H_{\rm der}^{{G^{\prime}}^{d}}(C^{\prime})

\displaystyle=H_{{\rm der},1}(C^{\prime})\times\cdots\times H_{{\rm der},d}(C^{\prime}).

By Corollary
2.3
, we have the following commutative diagram

\textstyle{((I_{\rm der})^{d}\cap\mathbb{J}_{b_{\bullet}})\backslash{\rm Irr}X_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{\lambda_{\bullet}}({b_{\bullet}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{\sim\hskip 8.19447pt\ \ }
\textstyle{{\rm Irr}(t^{\lambda_{\bullet}}K^{d}t^{\mu_{\bullet}}K^{d}t^{-{\lambda}_{\bullet}^{\dagger}}\cap(I^{d})_{(U^{d})_{\lambda_{\bullet}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{((I_{\rm der}^{\prime})^{d}\cap\mathbb{J}_{b_{\bullet}^{\prime}})\backslash{\rm Irr}X_{\mu_{\bullet}^{\prime}}^{{\lambda}_{\bullet}^{\prime}}(b_{\bullet}^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt\ }
\textstyle{{\rm Irr}(t^{{\lambda}_{\bullet}^{\prime}}{K^{\prime}}^{d}t^{\mu_{\bullet}^{\prime}}{K^{\prime}}^{d}t^{-{\lambda}_{\bullet}^{\prime{\dagger}}}\cap({I^{\prime}}^{d})_{(U^{d})_{{\lambda}_{\bullet}^{\prime}}}),}

where the right vertical bijection follows from Lemma
2.6
and the homeomorphism
f:{\rm Gr}_{G^{d}}^{{\omega_{\bullet}}}\cong{\rm Gr}_{{G^{\prime}}^{d}}^{\omega_{\bullet}^{\prime}}
.

Let
\mathbb{J}_{b_{\bullet}}^{0}
and
\mathbb{J}_{b_{\bullet}^{\prime}}^{0}
be the kernels of the natural projections
\mathbb{J}_{b_{\bullet}}\to\pi_{1}(G^{d})
and
\mathbb{J}_{b_{\bullet}^{\prime}}\to\pi_{1}({G^{\prime}}^{d})
respectively. Then we have a commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathbb{J}_{b_{\bullet}}^{0}\backslash{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu_{\bullet}}({b_{\bullet}})^{\omega_{\bullet}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\mathbb{J}_{b_{\bullet}}\backslash{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu_{\bullet}}({b_{\bullet}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{J}_{b_{\bullet}^{\prime}}^{0}\backslash{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu_{\bullet}^{\prime}}(b_{\bullet}^{\prime})^{\omega_{\bullet}^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\mathbb{J}_{b_{\bullet}^{\prime}}\backslash{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu_{\bullet}^{\prime}}(b_{\bullet}^{\prime}).}

Thus the bijection
\mathbb{J}_{b_{\bullet}}\backslash{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu_{\bullet}}({b_{\bullet}})\cong\mathbb{J}_{b_{\bullet}^{\prime}}\backslash{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu_{\bullet}^{\prime}}(b_{\bullet}^{\prime})
follows from the following commutative diagrams

\textstyle{((I_{\rm der})^{d}\cap\mathbb{J}_{b_{\bullet}})\backslash{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu_{\bullet}}({b_{\bullet}})^{\omega_{\bullet}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 8.19447pt\sim}
\textstyle{(I^{d}\cap\mathbb{J}_{b})\backslash{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu_{\bullet}}({b_{\bullet}})^{\omega_{\bullet}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 8.19447pt\sim}
\textstyle{\mathbb{J}_{b_{\bullet}}^{0}\backslash{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu_{\bullet}}({b_{\bullet}})^{\omega_{\bullet}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{((I_{\rm der}^{\prime})^{d}\cap\mathbb{J}_{b_{\bullet}^{\prime}})\backslash{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu_{\bullet}^{\prime}}(b_{\bullet}^{\prime})^{\omega_{\bullet}^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 8.19447pt\sim}
\textstyle{({I^{\prime}}^{d}\cap\mathbb{J}_{b_{\bullet}^{\prime}})\backslash{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu_{\bullet}^{\prime}}(b_{\bullet}^{\prime})^{\omega_{\bullet}^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 8.19447pt\sim}
\textstyle{\mathbb{J}_{b_{\bullet}^{\prime}}^{0}\backslash{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu_{\bullet}^{\prime}}(b_{\bullet}^{\prime})^{\omega_{\bullet}^{\prime}},}

where the left horizontal bijections follow from Corollary
2.3
; the right horizontal bijections follow from that
\mathbb{J}_{b_{\bullet}}^{0}=I^{d}\cap\mathbb{J}_{b_{\bullet}}
and
\mathbb{J}_{b_{\bullet}^{\prime}}^{0}={I^{\prime}}^{d}\cap\mathbb{J}_{b_{\bullet}^{\prime}}
as
{b_{\bullet}},b_{\bullet}^{\prime}
are superbasic; the leftmost vertical bijection follows from the natural bijection

((I_{\rm der})^{d}\cap\mathbb{J}_{b_{\bullet}})\backslash{\rm Irr}X_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{\lambda_{\bullet}}({b_{\bullet}})\cong((I_{\rm der}^{\prime})^{d}\cap\mathbb{J}_{b_{\bullet}^{\prime}})\backslash{\rm Irr}X_{\mu_{\bullet}^{\prime}}^{{\lambda}_{\bullet}^{\prime}}(b_{\bullet}^{\prime})

in the commutative diagram (a). The proof is finished.

∎

Let
G_{\rm ad}
denote the adjoint group of
G
. As
b
is superbasic, by
[
3
, Lemma 3.11]
,
G_{\rm ad}\cong\prod_{i}{\rm Res}_{F_{i}/F}{\rm PGL}_{n_{i}}
for some unramified extensions
F_{i}/F
. In view of the following natural central isogenies

G\longrightarrow G_{\rm ad}\cong\prod_{i}{\rm Res}_{F_{d_{i}}/F}{\rm PGL}_{n_{i}}\longleftarrow\prod_{i}{\rm Res}_{F_{d_{i}}/F}{\rm GL}_{n_{i}},

we will assume in the rest of this section that
G={\rm Res}_{E/F}{\rm GL}_{n}
for some unramified extension
E/F
by Lemma
3.2
.

3.3.
Reduction procedure in the minuscule case

Now we consider the case where
{\mu_{\bullet}}
is minuscule. Let
\mathcal{A}_{{\mu_{\bullet}},{b_{\bullet}}}=\mathcal{A}_{{\mu_{\bullet}},{b_{\bullet}}}^{G^{d}}
and
\mathcal{A}_{{\mu_{\bullet}},{b_{\bullet}}}^{\rm top}=\mathcal{A}_{{\mu_{\bullet}},{b_{\bullet}}}^{G^{d},{\rm top}}
be defined in §
2.2
. For
{\lambda_{\bullet}}\in\mathcal{A}_{{\mu_{\bullet}},{b_{\bullet}}}
set
{\lambda}_{\bullet}^{\dagger}={b_{\bullet}}\sigma_{\bullet}({\lambda_{\bullet}})
,
{\lambda}^{\natural}_{\bullet}=-{\lambda_{\bullet}}+{\lambda}_{\bullet}^{\dagger}
and
{\lambda}^{\flat}_{\bullet}=\epsilon_{\lambda_{\bullet}}^{-1}({\lambda}_{\bullet}^{\natural})
, where
\epsilon_{\lambda_{\bullet}}:=\epsilon_{\lambda_{\bullet}}^{G^{d}}
is defined in §
1.5
.

Since
{\mu_{\bullet}}
is minuscule, we identify
\mathbb{B}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{\widehat{G}^{d}}
canonically with the set of cocharacters in
Y^{d}
which are conjugate to
{\mu_{\bullet}}
. Moreover, for
\zeta_{\bullet}\in\mathbb{B}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{\widehat{G}^{d}}
, the corresponding Mirković-Vilonen cycle is
S_{\zeta_{\bullet}}=(K_{U})^{d}t^{\zeta_{\bullet}}K^{d}/K^{d}
.

Theorem 3.3

(
[
18
, Proposition 1.6]
)
.

Assume
{\mu_{\bullet}}
is minuscule. For
{\lambda}\in\mathcal{A}_{{\mu_{\bullet}},{b_{\bullet}}}
,

(1)
X_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{\lambda_{\bullet}}({b_{\bullet}})
is an affine space;

(2)
{\lambda_{\bullet}}\in\mathcal{A}_{{\mu_{\bullet}},{b_{\bullet}}}^{\rm top}
if and only if
{\lambda}^{\flat}_{\bullet}=\epsilon_{\lambda_{\bullet}}^{-1}({\lambda}_{\bullet}^{\natural})\in\mathbb{B}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{\widehat{G}^{d}}({\underline{\lambda}}_{G^{d}}({b_{\bullet}}))
.

Moreover, the maps
{\lambda_{\bullet}}\mapsto{\lambda}_{\bullet}^{\flat}
and
{\lambda_{\bullet}}\mapsto\overline{X_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{\lambda_{\bullet}}({b_{\bullet}})}
induce a bijection

\mathbb{J}_{b_{\bullet}}\backslash{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu_{\bullet}}({b_{\bullet}})\cong\mathbb{B}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{\widehat{G}^{d}}({\underline{\lambda}}_{G^{d}}({b_{\bullet}})).

As a consequence, for
C\in{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu_{\bullet}}({b_{\bullet}})
there exists
{\lambda_{\bullet}}\in\mathcal{A}_{{\mu_{\bullet}},{b_{\bullet}}}^{\rm top}
such that
\check{C}=\overline{X_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{\lambda_{\bullet}}({b_{\bullet}})}
. Define

\gamma^{G^{d}}(C)={\lambda}^{\flat}_{\bullet}\in\mathbb{B}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{\widehat{G}^{d}}({\underline{\lambda}}_{G^{d}}({b_{\bullet}})).

Moreover, we write

H({\lambda_{\bullet}}):=H^{G^{d}}(C)=t^{{\lambda_{\bullet}}}K^{d}t^{{\mu_{\bullet}}}K^{d}t^{-{\lambda}^{\dagger}_{\bullet}}\cap I^{d}=H_{1}({\lambda_{\bullet}})\times\cdots\times H_{d}({\lambda_{\bullet}}),

where
H_{\tau}({\lambda}):=H_{\tau}(C)
for
1\leqslant\tau\leqslant d
as in §
2.3
.

Remark 3.4
.

In
[
18
]
, the EL-charts for
X_{\mu_{\bullet}}({b_{\bullet}})
are parameterized by cocharacters
{\lambda_{\bullet}}
in
\mathcal{A}_{{\mu_{\bullet}},{b_{\bullet}}}\subseteq Y^{d}\cong(\mathbb{Z}^{n})^{dl}
with
l=\deg E/F
. By
[
18
, Corollary 4.18]
, the map, sending
{\lambda_{\bullet}}
to its
cotype
, induces a bijection

\mathbb{J}_{b_{\bullet}}\backslash{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu_{\bullet}}({b_{\bullet}})\cong\mathbb{B}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{\widehat{G}^{d}}({\underline{\lambda}}_{G^{d}}({b_{\bullet}})).

Following
[
18
, Definition 4.13]
, the cotype of
{\lambda_{\bullet}}
is equal to
\varepsilon_{\bullet}^{-1}({\lambda}_{\bullet}^{\natural})
, where
\varepsilon_{\bullet}
lies in
(W_{0})^{d}\cong(\mathfrak{S}_{n})^{dl}
such that

n{\lambda}_{i,j}(\varepsilon_{i,j}(k))+\varepsilon_{i,j}(k)<n{\lambda}_{i,j}(\varepsilon_{i,j}(k^{\prime}))+\varepsilon_{i,j}(k^{\prime})

for
1\leqslant i\leqslant d
,
1\leqslant j\leqslant l
, and
1\leqslant k^{\prime}<k\leqslant n
. This means that
({\lambda}_{i,j})_{\varepsilon_{i,j}(\alpha)}\geqslant 0
for all positive roots
\alpha
. So
\varepsilon_{\bullet}=\epsilon_{\lambda_{\bullet}}
and the cotype of
{\lambda_{\bullet}}
coincides with
{\lambda}_{\bullet}^{\flat}
.

By the definition of
\gamma^{G^{d}}
, the second statement of Theorem
3.1
(for the minuscule case) follows from Theorem
3.3
. It remains to show the first statement, which follows from the following result.

Proposition 3.5
.

Let
{\mu_{\bullet}}
be minuscule and let
{\lambda_{\bullet}}\in\mathcal{A}_{{\mu_{\bullet}},{b_{\bullet}}}^{\rm top}
. For
1\leqslant a\leqslant c\leqslant d
,

\displaystyle\quad\ \overline{t^{-{\lambda}_{a}}(H_{a}({\lambda_{\bullet}})t^{{\lambda}_{a}^{\dagger}}\times_{K}\cdots\times_{K}t^{-{\lambda}_{c}}H_{c}({\lambda_{\bullet}})t^{{\lambda}_{c}^{\dagger}}K/K}

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{{\lambda}_{a}}\overline{K_{U}t^{{\lambda}^{\flat}_{a}}\times_{K}\cdots\times_{K}K_{U}t^{{\lambda}^{\flat}_{c}}K/K}

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{{\lambda}_{a}}\overline{S_{{\lambda}_{a}^{\flat}}\tilde{\times}\cdots\tilde{\times}S_{{\lambda}_{c}^{\flat}}},

where
{\lambda}_{\bullet}^{\flat}=({\lambda}_{1}^{\flat},\dots,{\lambda}_{d}^{\flat})
and
{\lambda}_{\bullet}^{\dagger}=({\lambda}_{1}^{\dagger},\dots,{\lambda}_{d}^{\dagger})
.

As
G={\rm Res}_{E/F}{\rm GL}_{n}
, we have
G^{d}({\breve{F}})=\prod_{i=1}^{dl}G_{i}({\breve{F}})
, where
l=\deg E/F
and each
G_{i}
is isomorphic to
{\rm GL}_{n}
over
E
. Moreover,
\sigma_{\bullet}
sends
G_{i}
to
G_{i-1}
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant dl
with
G_{dl+1}=G_{1}
. By Lemma
2.9
(1), we see that
H({\lambda_{\bullet}})
only depends on
{\lambda_{\bullet}}\in\mathcal{A}_{{\mu_{\bullet}},{b_{\bullet}}}^{\rm top}
, the image of
{b_{\bullet}}
in
(\Omega_{G})^{d}
and the induced action of
\sigma_{\bullet}
on the root system. Moreover, by Corollary
2.4
, we can assume, by replacing
b
with a suitable
\Omega
-
\sigma
-conjugate, that

{b_{\bullet}}=(1,\dots,1,b)\in\prod_{i=1}^{dl}G_{i}({\breve{F}}).

Let
G^{\prime}={\rm GL}_{n}
and let
\sigma_{\bullet}^{\prime}
be the Frobenius automorphism of
(G^{\prime})^{dl}
defined in §
1.6
. Via the natural identification (over
E
)

(G^{\prime})^{dl}=\prod_{i=1}^{dl}G_{i}=G^{d},

we see that the induced actions of
\sigma_{\bullet}^{\prime}
and
\sigma_{\bullet}
on the root system coincide. Thus Proposition
3.5
for the triple
(G={\rm Res}_{E/F}{\rm GL}_{n},d,{b_{\bullet}})
is a consequence of its counterpart for the triple
(G^{\prime}={\rm GL}_{n},dl,{b_{\bullet}})
. So we will assume that
G={\rm GL}_{n}
when
{\mu_{\bullet}}
is minuscule.

3.4.
The minuscule case with
G={\rm GL}_{n}

Assume
G={\rm GL}_{n}
. Let
T
and
B
be the group of diagonal matrices and the group of upper triangular matrices respectively. Let
V=\oplus_{i=1}^{n}{\breve{F}}e_{i}
be the natural representation of
G({\breve{F}})
. Then there are natural identifications
X=\oplus_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{Z}e_{i}
,
Y=\oplus_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{Z}e_{i}^{\vee}
and
W_{0}=\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, where
(e_{i}^{\vee})_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n}
is the dual basis to
(e_{i})_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n}
and
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
denotes the symmetric group. Then the natural action of
w\in W_{0}
on
X
is given by
w(e_{i})=e_{w(i)}
. Moreover, we have
\Phi=\{\alpha_{i,j}=e_{i}-e_{j};1\leqslant i\neq j\leqslant n\}
and the simple roots are
\alpha_{i}=e_{i}-e_{i+1}
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant n-1
. Notice that
\Omega
is a free abelian group of rank one. We fix a generator
\omega\in\Omega
which sends
e_{i}
to
e_{i+1}
for
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, where
e_{j+n}=te_{j}
for
j\in\mathbb{Z}
. So we can assume
b=\omega^{m}
for some
m\in\mathbb{Z}
. As
b
is superbasic,
m
is coprime to
n
. The embedding
h\mapsto(1,\dots,1,h)
induces an identification
Y=Y_{\sigma}\cong(Y^{d})_{\sigma_{\bullet}}
, through which we have
{\underline{\lambda}}_{G}(b)={\underline{\lambda}}_{G^{d}}({b_{\bullet}})
.

Suppose
{\mu_{\bullet}}\in Y^{d}
is minuscule. Let
{\lambda_{\bullet}}=({\lambda}_{1},\dots,{\lambda}_{d})\in\mathcal{A}_{{\mu_{\bullet}},{b_{\bullet}}}
. Following §
3.3
we can define
{\lambda}_{\bullet}^{\dagger}=({\lambda}_{1}^{\dagger},\dots,{\lambda}_{d}^{\dagger})
,
{\lambda}_{\bullet}^{\natural}=({\lambda}_{1}^{\natural},\dots,{\lambda}_{d}^{\natural})
,
{\lambda}_{\bullet}^{\flat}=({\lambda}_{1}^{\flat},\dots,{\lambda}_{d}^{\flat})
and
H({\lambda_{\bullet}})=H_{1}({\lambda_{\bullet}})\times\cdots\times H_{d}({\lambda_{\bullet}})
. Notice that
{\lambda}_{\tau}^{\dagger}={\lambda}_{\tau+1}
for
1\leqslant\tau\leqslant d-1
.

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
{\lambda_{\bullet}}\in\mathcal{A}_{{\mu_{\bullet}},{b_{\bullet}}}
and
1\leqslant a\leqslant c\leqslant d
. Then

t^{-{\lambda}_{a}}H_{a}({\lambda_{\bullet}})t^{{\lambda}^{\dagger}_{a}}\times_{K}\cdots\times_{K}t^{-{\lambda}_{c}}H_{c}({\lambda_{\bullet}})t^{{\lambda}^{\dagger}_{c}}K=K_{U_{{\lambda}_{a}}}t^{{\lambda}^{\natural}_{a}}\times_{K}\cdots\times_{K}K_{U_{{\lambda}_{c}}}t^{{\lambda}^{\natural}_{c}}K.

Proof.

Let
R_{{\mu_{\bullet}},{b_{\bullet}}}({\lambda_{\bullet}})=\sqcup_{\tau=1}^{d}R_{\tau}({\lambda_{\bullet}})\subseteq\sqcup_{\tau=1}^{d}\Phi
be as in §
2.2
, where

R_{\tau}({\lambda_{\bullet}})=\{\alpha\in\Phi;({\lambda}_{\tau})_{\alpha}\geqslant 1,\langle\alpha,{\lambda}^{\natural}_{\tau}\rangle=\langle\alpha,-{\lambda}_{\tau}+{\lambda}_{\tau}^{\dagger}\rangle=-1\}.

By the proof of Proposition
2.9
, we have
H_{\tau}({\lambda_{\bullet}})=I_{{\lambda}_{\tau}}\Sigma_{\tau}
, where

\Sigma_{\tau}=\prod_{\alpha\in R_{\tau}({\lambda_{\bullet}})}U_{\alpha}(t^{\langle\alpha,{\lambda}_{\tau}\rangle-1}\mathcal{O}_{{\breve{F}}}).

Thus
({\lambda}_{\tau}^{\dagger})_{\alpha}=({\lambda}_{\tau})_{\alpha}-1\geqslant 0
and
U_{\alpha}(t^{\langle\alpha,{\lambda}_{\tau}\rangle-1}\mathcal{O}_{{\breve{F}}})=U_{\alpha}(t^{\langle\alpha,{\lambda}^{\dagger}_{\tau}\rangle}\mathcal{O}_{{\breve{F}}})\subseteq I_{{\lambda}^{\dagger}_{\tau}}^{+}
for
\alpha\in R_{\tau}({\lambda_{\bullet}})
, which means
\Sigma_{\tau}\subseteq I_{{\lambda}^{\dagger}_{\tau}}^{+}
for
1\leqslant\tau\leqslant d
. As
{\lambda}^{\dagger}_{\tau}={\lambda}_{\tau+1}
for
1\leqslant\tau\leqslant d-1
, we have

\displaystyle\quad\ t^{-{\lambda}_{a}}H_{a}({\lambda_{\bullet}})t^{{\lambda}_{a}^{\dagger}}\times_{K}\cdots\times_{K}t^{-{\lambda}_{c-1}}H_{c-1}t^{{\lambda}^{\dagger}_{c-1}}\times_{K}t^{-{\lambda}_{c}}H_{c}({\lambda_{\bullet}})t^{{\lambda}^{\dagger}_{c}}K

\displaystyle=t^{-{\lambda}_{a}}H_{a}({\lambda_{\bullet}})t^{{\lambda}_{c+1}}\times_{K}\cdots\times_{K}t^{-{\lambda}_{c-1}}H_{c-1}({\lambda_{\bullet}})t^{{\lambda}_{c}}\times_{K}t^{-{\lambda}_{c}}H_{c}({\lambda_{\bullet}})t^{{\lambda}^{\dagger}_{c}}K

\displaystyle=t^{-{\lambda}_{a}}I_{{\lambda}_{a}}\Sigma_{a}t^{{\lambda}_{a+1}}\times_{K}\cdots\times_{K}t^{-{\lambda}_{c-1}}I_{{\lambda}_{c-1}}\Sigma_{c-1}t^{{\lambda}_{c}}\times_{K}t^{-{\lambda}_{c}}I_{{\lambda}_{c}}\Sigma_{c}t^{{\lambda}^{\dagger}_{c}}K

\displaystyle=t^{-{\lambda}_{a}}I_{{\lambda}_{a}}t^{{\lambda}_{a+1}}\times_{K}\cdots\times_{K}t^{-{\lambda}_{c-1}}I_{{\lambda}_{c-1}}t^{{\lambda}_{c}}\times_{K}t^{-{\lambda}_{c}}I_{{\lambda}_{c}}t^{{\lambda}^{\dagger}_{c}}K

\displaystyle=t^{-{\lambda}_{a}}I_{{\lambda}_{a}}^{+}I_{{\lambda}_{a}}^{-}t^{{\lambda}_{a+1}}\times_{K}\cdots\times_{K}t^{-{\lambda}_{c-1}}I_{{\lambda}_{c-1}}^{+}I_{{\lambda}_{c-1}}^{-}t^{{\lambda}_{c}}\times_{K}(t^{-{\lambda}_{c}}I_{{\lambda}_{c}}^{+}I_{{\lambda}_{c}}^{-}t^{{\lambda}^{\dagger}_{c}})K
