is invariant for
\beta\in\mathcal{O}_{-\alpha_{i}}\subseteq\Phi_{N_{v}}
. Otherwise, there exists
\xi\in\mathcal{O}_{-\alpha_{i}}
such that
\langle\xi,{\lambda}^{\natural}\rangle={\lambda}_{\xi^{-1}}-{\lambda}_{\xi}\neq 0
(see Lemma
2.7
). Since
{\lambda}^{\natural}
is minuscule and

\sum_{\beta\in\mathcal{O}_{-\alpha_{i}}}\langle\beta,{\lambda}^{\natural}\rangle=\sum_{\beta\in\mathcal{O}_{-\alpha_{i}}}{\lambda}_{\beta^{-1}}-{\lambda}_{\beta}=0,

there exists
\gamma\in\mathcal{O}_{-\alpha_{i}}
such that
\langle\gamma,{\lambda}^{\natural}\rangle={\lambda}_{\gamma^{-1}}-{\lambda}_{\gamma}=-1
. On the other hand, we have
{\lambda}_{\gamma^{-1}}\geqslant 0
since
{\lambda}\in\mathcal{A}_{\mu,b}^{\rm top}(v)
and
\gamma^{-1}\in\mathcal{O}_{-\alpha_{i}}\subseteq\Phi_{N_{v}}
. So
\gamma\in R({\lambda})
, which contradicts that
R({\lambda})\cap\mathcal{O}_{-\alpha_{i}}=\emptyset
. The claim is proved.

Let
c_{i}={\lambda}_{\beta}=-{\lambda}_{-\beta}-1\in\mathbb{Z}
for
\beta\in\mathcal{O}_{-\alpha_{i}}
, which is a constant by the above claim. Let
\psi_{i}=\sum_{\delta\in\mathcal{O}_{\alpha_{i}}}{\lambda}_{\delta}\delta^{\vee}=(-c_{i}-1)\sum_{\delta\in\mathcal{O}_{\alpha_{i}}}\delta^{\vee}
and
x_{i}=t^{\psi_{i}}s_{H_{i}}
. Then
p(x_{i})=p(s_{H_{i}})
and
t^{\psi_{i}}\in W^{a}\cap\mathbb{J}_{b}
. Moreover, by Lemma
1.3
(2) and that
\mathcal{O}_{\alpha_{i}}
is orthogonal (see Lemma
5.14
) we have

x_{i}({\lambda})=\psi_{i}+(\prod_{\delta\in\mathcal{O}_{\alpha_{i}}}s_{\tilde{\delta}})({\lambda})=\psi_{i}+{\lambda}-\sum_{\delta\in\mathcal{O}_{\alpha_{i}}}{\lambda}_{\delta}\delta^{\vee}={\lambda}.

Thus
x_{i}
satisfies our requirements.

Let
y={\tilde{w}}x_{r}\cdots x_{1}\in\tilde{W}\cap\mathbb{J}_{b}
. Then it follows that
y({\lambda})={\tilde{w}}({\lambda})={\lambda}^{\prime}
and
p(y)(v)=p({\tilde{w}})p(s_{H_{r}})\cdots p(s_{H_{1}})(v)=v
as desired.
∎

Lemma 5.16
.

Let
{\lambda}\in\mathcal{A}_{\mu,b}^{\rm top}(v)
and
{\tilde{w}}\in\Omega_{M_{b}}\cap\mathbb{J}_{b}
. Then
{\tilde{w}}({\lambda})\in\mathcal{A}_{\mu,b}
. Moreover, if
{\tilde{w}}({\lambda})\in\mathcal{A}_{\mu,b}(v)
, then
{\tilde{w}}({\lambda})\in\mathcal{A}_{\mu,b}^{\rm top}(v)
and
{\lambda}\sim{\tilde{w}}({\lambda})
.

Proof.

Let
\chi={\tilde{w}}({\lambda})
. By Lemma
2.7
,
\chi^{\natural}\in W_{M_{b}}({\lambda}^{\natural})
and hence
\chi\in\mathcal{A}_{\mu,b}
by Proposition
2.9
. As
{\tilde{w}}\in\Omega_{M_{b}}\cap\mathbb{J}_{b}
, it follows the same way as Lemma
2.8
that
p({\tilde{w}})(R({\lambda})\cap\Phi_{M_{b}})=R(\chi)\cap\Phi_{M_{b}}
. Combining Proposition
2.9
with Lemma
5.4
we have

(a)

\displaystyle\dim X_{z(\chi^{\natural})}^{z(\chi),M}(b_{M})=|R(\chi)\cap\Phi_{M_{b}}|=|R({\lambda})\cap\Phi_{M_{b}}|=\dim X_{z({\lambda}^{\natural})}^{M}(b_{M})=\dim X_{z(\chi^{\natural})}^{M}(b_{M}),

where the third equality follows from Corollary
5.5
(1) by noticing that
{\lambda}\in\mathcal{A}_{\mu,b}^{\rm top}
, and the last one follows from that
z(\chi^{\natural}),z({\lambda}^{\natural})
are conjugate by
W_{M}
.

Suppose
\chi\in\mathcal{A}_{\mu,b}(v)
. Then
\chi_{\beta}\geqslant 0
and
\chi_{-\beta}=-\chi_{\beta}-1\leqslant-1
for
\beta\in\Phi_{N_{v}}
, which together with (a) implies that
\chi\in\mathcal{A}_{\mu,b}^{\rm top}
by Corollary
5.5
.

For
\eta\in\mathcal{A}_{\mu,b}(v)
we have
-\langle\beta,\eta\rangle\geqslant\eta_{-\beta}\geqslant 0
for
\beta\in\Phi_{N_{-v}}=-\Phi_{N_{v}}
. Then

It^{\eta}K/K=I_{M_{b}}I_{N_{v}}I_{N_{-v}}t^{\eta}K/K=I_{M_{b}}I_{N_{v}}t^{\eta}K/K,

which implies that
t^{nv}It^{\eta}K/K\subseteq It^{nv+\eta}K/K
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
. In particular,
t^{nv}X_{\mu}^{\chi}(b)\subseteq X_{\mu}^{nv+\chi}(b)
and hence
\chi\sim nv+\chi
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
. Choose
n
sufficiently large so that
{\tilde{w}}t^{nv}I_{N_{v}}t^{-nv}\subseteq I_{N_{v}}{\tilde{w}}
. Then

\displaystyle{\tilde{w}}t^{nv}X_{\mu}^{\lambda}(b)

\displaystyle\subseteq{\tilde{w}}t^{nv}It^{\lambda}K/K

\displaystyle={\tilde{w}}t^{nv}I_{M_{b}}I_{N_{v}}t^{\lambda}K/K

\displaystyle=I_{M_{b}}{\tilde{w}}(t^{nv}I_{N_{v}}t^{-nv})t^{nv+{\lambda}}K/K

\displaystyle\subseteq I_{M_{b}}I_{N_{v}}{\tilde{w}}t^{nv+{\lambda}}K/K

\displaystyle\subseteq It^{nv+\chi}K/K.

Therefore,
{\tilde{w}}t^{nv}X_{\mu}^{\lambda}(b)\subseteq X_{\mu}^{nv+\chi}(b)
and hence
{\lambda}\sim nv+\chi\sim\chi
as desired.
∎

Combining Lemma
5.15
with Lemma
5.16
we have

Corollary 5.17
.

Let
{\lambda},{\lambda}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{\mu,b}^{\rm top}(v)
. Then
{\lambda}\sim{\lambda}^{\prime}
if and only if
{\lambda}^{\prime}={\tilde{w}}({\lambda})
for some
{\tilde{w}}\in\Omega_{M_{b}}\cap\mathbb{J}_{b}
.

5.7.
End of the proof

Let
v
,
z
,
M
,
b_{M}
be as in §
5.2
. Recall that
I_{\mu,M}
is the set of
W_{M}
-orbits of
W_{0}(\mu)
, and
I_{\mu,b_{M},M}=\{\eta\in I_{\mu,M};\kappa_{M}(t^{\eta})=\kappa_{M}(b_{M})\}
. Let
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu,b}^{\rm top}(v)
denote the set of equivalence classes of
\mathcal{A}_{\mu,b}^{\rm top}(v)
with respect to
\sim
defined in §
5.4
. Similarly, we can define an equivalence relation
\sim_{M}
on
\mathcal{A}_{\eta,b_{M}}^{M,{\rm top}}
for
\eta\in I_{\mu,b_{M},M}
, and denote by
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{\eta,b_{M}}^{M,{\rm top}}
the set of corresponding equivalence classes. As
b_{M}
is superbasic in
M({\breve{F}})
, we have
\chi\sim_{M}\chi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{\eta,b_{M}}^{M,{\rm top}}
if and only if
\chi^{\prime}={\tilde{w}}(\chi)
for some
{\tilde{w}}\in\Omega_{M}\cap\mathbb{J}_{b_{M}}^{M}
.

Proof of Proposition
4.5
.

We show that there are bijections

\mathbb{J}_{b}\backslash{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu}(b)\overset{\Psi_{1}}{\longleftarrow}\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu,b}^{\rm top}(v)\overset{\Psi_{2}}{\longrightarrow}\sqcup_{\eta\in I_{\mu,b_{M},M}}\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{\eta,b_{M}}^{M,{\rm top}},

where
\Psi_{1}
and
\Psi_{2}
are given by
{\lambda}\mapsto\mathbb{J}_{b}\overline{{\rm Irr}X_{\mu}^{\lambda}(b)}
and
{\lambda}\mapsto z({\lambda})
respectively. Indeed, by Proposition
2.9
and Lemma
5.10
we see that
\Psi_{1}
is bijective.

Let
{\lambda}\in\mathcal{A}_{\mu,b}^{\rm top}(v)
. By Corollary
5.5
,
z({\lambda})\in\mathcal{A}_{z({\lambda}^{\natural}),b_{M}}^{M,{\rm top}}
and
z({\lambda}^{\natural})\in I_{\mu,b_{M},M}
. Moreover, by Lemma
2.7
and Corollary
5.17
we deduce that

{\lambda}\sim{\lambda}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{\mu,b}^{\rm top}(v)\Leftrightarrow z({\lambda})\sim_{M}z({\lambda}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{A}_{z({\lambda}^{\natural}),b_{M}}^{M,{\rm top}}.

So
\Psi_{2}
is well defined. On the other hand, let
\chi\in\mathcal{A}_{\eta,b_{M}}^{M,{\rm top}}
with
\eta\in I_{\mu,b_{M},M}
. By Corollary
5.5
and
5.17
, the map
\chi\to nv+z^{-1}(\chi)
with
n\gg 0
induces the inverse map of
\Psi_{2}
. So
\Psi_{2}
is also bijective.

Therefore,

\displaystyle|\mathbb{J}_{b}\backslash{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu}(b)|

\displaystyle=\sum_{\eta\in I_{\mu,b_{M},M}}|\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{\eta,b_{M}}^{M,{\rm top}}|;

\displaystyle=\sum_{\eta\in I_{\mu,b_{M},M}}\dim V_{\eta}^{\widehat{M}}({\underline{\lambda}}_{M}(b_{M}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{\eta\in I_{\mu,M}}\dim V_{\eta}^{\widehat{M}}({\underline{\lambda}}_{M}(b_{M}))

\displaystyle=\dim V_{\mu}({\underline{\lambda}}_{M}(b_{M}))

\displaystyle=\dim V_{\mu}({\underline{\lambda}}_{G}(b)),

where the second equality follows from
[
18
, Theorem 1.5]
; the fourth one follows from that
V_{\mu}=\oplus_{\eta\in I_{\mu,M}}V_{\eta}^{\widehat{M}}
as
\mu
is minuscule. The proof is finished.
∎

6.
The stabilizer in
\mathbb{J}_{b}

In this section, we give an algorithm to compute the stabilizer
N_{\mathbb{J}_{b}}(C)
of
C\in{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu}(b)
in
\mathbb{J}_{b}
.

6.1.
Reduction to the adjoint case

Let
G_{\rm ad}
be the adjoint quotient of
G
. By
[
3
, §2]
, the natural projection
f:G\to G_{\rm ad}
induces a Cartesian square

\textstyle{X_{\mu}(b)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{X_{\mu_{\rm ad}}(b_{\rm ad})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}(G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}(G_{\rm ad}),}

where the vertical maps are the natural projections;
\mu_{\rm ad}
and
b_{\rm ad}
are the images of
\mu
and
b
under
f
respectively. In particular, the stabilizer
N_{\mathbb{J}_{b}}(C)
can be computed from the stabilizer
N_{\mathbb{J}_{b_{\rm ad}}}(C_{\rm ad})
of
C_{\rm ad}=f(C)
in
\mathbb{J}_{b_{\rm ad}}
via the following natural Cartesian square

\textstyle{N_{\mathbb{J}_{b}}(C)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{N_{\mathbb{J}_{b_{\rm ad}}}(C_{\rm ad})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{J}_{b}^{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\mathbb{J}_{b_{\rm ad}},}

where the vertical maps are the natural inclusions, and
\mathbb{J}_{b}^{0}
is the kernel of the natural projection
\mathbb{J}_{b}\to\pi_{1}(G)
. Thus we can assume
G
is adjoint and simple.

6.2.
Reduction to the basic case

Now we show how to pass to the case where
b
is basic. Let
P=MN
and
\beta:X_{\mu}(b)\to{\rm Gr}_{M}
be as in §
2.1
such that
M
is the centralizer of
\nu_{G}(b)
. In particular,
\mathbb{J}_{b}=\mathbb{J}_{b}^{M}
. Let
\eta\in I_{\mu,b,M}
such that
X_{\eta}^{M}(b)
contains an open dense subset of
\beta(C)
. Let

C^{M}=\overline{\beta(C)\cap X_{\eta}^{M}(b)}\subseteq X_{\eta}^{M}(b).

By Proposition
4.3
(1) and (4),
C=\overline{X_{\mu}^{Z^{N},C^{M}}(b)}
for some
Z^{N}\in\Sigma_{\mu,\eta}^{N}
. Note that
jX_{\mu}^{Z^{N},C^{M}}(b)=X_{\mu}^{Z^{N},jC^{M}}(b)
for
j\in\mathbb{J}_{b}^{M}=\mathbb{J}_{b}
. So we have
N_{\mathbb{J}_{b}}(C)=N_{\mathbb{J}_{b}^{M}}(C^{M})
.

6.3.
Reduction to the minuscule case

Assume
b
is basic. If
G
has no nonzero minuscule cocharacters, then
b
is unramified and
N_{\mathbb{J}_{b}}(C)
is determined in
[
56
, Theorem 4.4.14]
. Otherwise, by Lemma
4.6
, there exists a dominant minuscule cocharacter
{\mu_{\bullet}}\in Y^{d}
for some
d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 1}
such that
\mathbb{B}_{\mu}^{\widehat{G}}
occurs in

\mathbb{B}_{\mu_{\bullet}}^{\widehat{G}}=\mathbb{B}_{\mu_{1}}^{\widehat{G}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbb{B}_{\mu_{d}}^{\widehat{G}}.

Let
X_{\mu_{\bullet}}({b_{\bullet}})
be as in §
1.6
. By Theorem
0.7
, there exists
C^{\prime}\in{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu_{\bullet}}({b_{\bullet}})
such that

\overline{{\rm pr}(C^{\prime})}=\overline{C}\subseteq{\rm Gr},

and moreover, the map
g\mapsto(g,\dots,g)
gives an isomorphism
N_{\mathbb{J}_{b}}(C)\cong N_{\mathbb{J}_{b_{\bullet}}}(C^{\prime})
.

6.4.
Small cocharacters

In the rest of the section we assume that
G=G_{\rm ad}
is simple,
{\mu_{\bullet}}
is minuscule and
b
is basic. By abuse of notation, we write
X_{\mu}(b)
for
X_{\mu_{\bullet}}({b_{\bullet}})
by assuming that
\mu
is minuscule in the rest of this section. Then we can adopt the notation in §
5
.

Let
v\in V^{p(b\sigma)}_{\rm gen}\cap Y
. For
D\subseteq\Phi
we set
D(v,+)=\{\alpha\in\Phi;\langle\alpha,v\rangle>0\}
. We say
{\lambda}\in\mathcal{A}_{\mu,b}^{\rm top}
is
v
-small if
{\lambda}\in\mathcal{A}_{\mu,b}^{\rm top}(v)
(see §
5.4
) and for each
\alpha\in\Pi(v,+)
(see §
1.2
) there exists
\beta\in\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}
such that
{\lambda}_{\beta}=0
. We say
v
is permissible if
v
-small cocharacters exist.

We say
{\lambda}\in\mathcal{A}_{\mu,b}^{\rm top}
is small if it is
v
-small for some
v\in V^{p(b\sigma)}_{\rm gen}\cap Y
, and we define
\Pi({\lambda})
to be the set of roots
\alpha\in\Pi-\Phi_{M_{b}}
such that
{\lambda}_{\beta}\geqslant 0
for some/any
\beta\in\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}
(see Corollary
5.5
). By definition,
\Pi({\lambda})=\Pi(v,+)
if
{\lambda}
is
v
-small.

Lemma 6.1
.

If
{\lambda}\in\mathcal{A}_{\mu,b}^{\rm top}
is not small, then there exists
\alpha\in\Pi
such that
W_{\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{\alpha}}}
is finite and
{\lambda}_{\beta}\geqslant 1
for
\beta\in\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}

Proof.

By Proposition
5.12
, there exists
v\in V^{p(b\sigma)}_{\rm gen}\cap Y
such that
{\lambda}\in\mathcal{A}_{\mu,b}^{\rm top}(v)
. As
{\lambda}
is not
v
-small, there exists
\alpha\in\Pi(v,+)-\Phi_{M_{b}}
such that
{\lambda}_{\beta}\geqslant 1
for
\beta\in\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}
. Moreover,
W_{\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{\alpha}}}
is finite by Lemma
5.3
.
∎

Proposition 6.2
.

For each
C\in{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu}(b)
there exists small
{\lambda}\in\mathcal{A}_{\mu,b}^{\rm top}
such that
C\in\mathbb{J}_{b}{\rm Irr}\overline{X_{\mu}^{\lambda}(b)}
.

Proof.

Recall the dominance order
\leq
on
Y
defined in §
1.1
. For
\eta,\chi\in Y
write
\eta\unlhd\chi
if either
\eta\lneq\chi
(see §
1.1
for
\leq
) or
\eta\in W_{0}(\chi)
and
\chi\leqslant\eta
. Let
{\lambda}
be a minimal cocharacter in the set

\{\chi\in\mathcal{A}_{\mu,b}^{\rm top};C\in\mathbb{J}_{b}{\rm Irr}\overline{X_{\mu}^{\chi}(b)}\}

under the partial order
\unlhd
. We show that
{\lambda}
is small.

Suppose
{\lambda}
is not small. Let
\alpha\in\Pi
as in Lemma
6.1
, and let
{\tilde{w}}\in\mathbb{J}_{b}
be the maximal element of
W_{\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{\alpha}}}
. By Lemma
5.1
,

{\tilde{w}}=\prod_{\beta\in\mathcal{O}_{\gamma}}s_{\tilde{\beta}},

where
\mathcal{O}_{\gamma}
is orthogonal with
\gamma=\alpha
if
\langle\alpha^{d},\alpha^{\vee}\rangle\neq-1
, and
\gamma=\alpha^{d}+\alpha
otherwise. In particular,
{\lambda}_{\beta}\geqslant 1
for
\beta\in\mathcal{O}_{\gamma}
. Let
{\lambda}^{\prime}={\tilde{w}}({\lambda})
. By Corollary
5.9
,
{\lambda}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{\mu,b}^{\rm top}
and
C\in\mathbb{J}_{b}{\rm Irr}\overline{X_{\mu}^{\lambda}(b)}=\mathbb{J}_{b}{\rm Irr}\overline{X_{\mu}^{{\lambda}^{\prime}}(b)}
. Moreover, as
\mathcal{O}_{\gamma}
is orthogonal,

{\lambda}^{\prime}={\tilde{w}}({\lambda})=p({\tilde{w}})({\lambda}-\sum_{\beta\in\Phi^{+}\cap\mathcal{O}_{\gamma}}\beta^{\vee}).

If
\Phi^{+}\cap\mathcal{O}_{\gamma}\neq\emptyset
, we have
{\lambda}^{\prime}\lneq{\lambda}
since
\langle\beta,{\lambda}\rangle={\lambda}_{\beta}+1\geqslant 2
for
\beta\in\Phi^{+}\cap\mathcal{O}_{\gamma}
. Otherwise,

{\lambda}^{\prime}=p({\tilde{w}})({\lambda})={\lambda}-\sum_{\beta\in\mathcal{O}_{\gamma}}{\lambda}_{\beta}\beta^{\vee}>{\lambda}.

Thus, in either case we have
{\lambda}^{\prime}\lhd{\lambda}
, contradicting the choice of
{\lambda}
. So
{\lambda}
is small as desired.
∎

We say a root
\alpha\in\Phi(v,+)
is indecomposable (in
\Phi(v,+)
) if it is not a sum of roots in
\Phi(v,+)\setminus\{\alpha\}
.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
v\in V^{p(b\sigma)}_{\rm gen}\cap Y
be permissible. Then each root of
\Pi(v,+)
is indecomposable in
\Phi(v,+)
.

Proof.

For
\alpha\in\Pi(v,+)
set
Y^{\prime}(v,\alpha)=\{{\lambda}\in Y;{\lambda}_{\alpha}=0,{\lambda}_{\beta}\geqslant 0\text{ for }\beta\in\Phi(v,+)\}
. We claim that

(a)
Y^{\prime}(v,\alpha)\neq\emptyset
.

By assumption, there is a
v
-small cocharacter
\chi
. By definition,
\chi\in Y^{\prime}(v,\gamma)
for some
\gamma\in\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}
. By Lemma
1.3
(3),
Y^{\prime}(v,\alpha)
and
Y^{\prime}(v,\gamma)
are conjugate by
\langle b\sigma\rangle
. So (a) is proved.

Suppose
\alpha=\sum_{i\in D}\alpha_{i}
for some
\alpha\neq\alpha_{i}\in\Phi(v,+)
. Let
{\lambda}\in Y^{\prime}(v,\alpha)
by (a). Then
{\lambda}_{\alpha}=0
and
\langle\alpha_{i},{\lambda}\rangle\geqslant{\lambda}_{\alpha_{i}}\geqslant 0
for
i\in D
. If
\alpha<0
is a minus simple root, then there exists
i_{0}\in D
such that
\alpha_{i_{0}}>0
. Hence
{\lambda}_{\alpha}=\langle\alpha,{\lambda}\rangle\geqslant\langle\alpha_{i_{0}},{\lambda}\rangle={\lambda}_{\alpha_{i_{0}}}+1\geqslant 1
, which is a contradiction. If
\alpha>0
is the highest root, then there exist
i_{1}\neq i_{2}\in D
such that
\alpha_{i_{1}},\alpha_{i_{2}}>0
. Again we have
\langle\alpha,{\lambda}\rangle\geqslant\langle\alpha_{i_{1}},{\lambda}\rangle+\langle\alpha_{i_{2}},{\lambda}\rangle\geqslant 2
and hence
{\lambda}_{\alpha}\geqslant 1
, which is also a contradiction. So
\alpha
is indecomposable as desired.
∎

Let
J=p(b\sigma)(J)\subseteq\Pi
such that the corresponding parabolic subgroup
W_{J}
(generated by
s_{{\tilde{\alpha}}}
for
\alpha\in J
) is finite. By a standard parahoric subgroup of type
J
we mean a subgroup of
\mathbb{J}_{b}
generated by
I\cap\mathbb{J}_{b}
and
W_{J}\cap\mathbb{J}_{b}
. We say a standard parahoric subgroup of type
J
is of maximal length if the length, of the maximal element of
W_{J}
, is maximal among all standard parahoric subgroups of
\mathbb{J}_{b}
.
The following result will be proved in Appendix
A
.

Proposition 6.4
.

If
v\in V^{p(b\sigma)}_{\rm gen}\cap Y
is permissible, then the parahoric subgroup of type
\Pi(v,+)
is of maximal length.

6.5.
Irreducibility implies smallness

Suppose
{\lambda}\in\mathcal{A}_{\mu,b}^{\rm top}
is not small. Let
\alpha\in\Pi
be as in Lemma
6.1
, and let
{\tilde{w}}\in W_{\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{\alpha}}}
be the longest element. Suppose
X_{\mu}^{\lambda}(b)
is irreducible. By Lemma
5.6
, Lemma
5.8
and Corollary
5.9
(2),
{\lambda}\neq{\tilde{w}}({\lambda})\in\mathcal{A}_{\mu,b}^{\rm top}
and
{\tilde{w}}X_{\mu}^{{\tilde{w}}({\lambda})}(b)\subseteq X_{\mu}^{\lambda}(b)
. Hence
{\tilde{w}}\overline{X_{\mu}^{{\tilde{w}}({\lambda})}(b)}=\overline{X_{\mu}^{\lambda}(b)}
is also irreducible. In particular,

{\tilde{w}}N_{\mathbb{J}_{b}}(\overline{X_{\mu}^{{\tilde{w}}({\lambda})}(b)})){\tilde{w}}^{-1}=N_{\mathbb{J}_{b}}(\overline{X_{\mu}^{\lambda}(b)}).

Notice that
{\tilde{w}}\in W^{a}\cap\mathbb{J}_{b}
and
N_{\mathbb{J}_{b}}(\overline{X_{\mu}^{{\tilde{w}}({\lambda})}(b)})),N_{\mathbb{J}_{b}}(\overline{X_{\mu}^{\lambda}(b)})
are both standard parahoric subgroups containing
I\cap\mathbb{J}_{b}
. Thus
{\tilde{w}}\in N_{\mathbb{J}_{b}}(\overline{X_{\mu}^{{\tilde{w}}({\lambda})}(b)}))=N_{\mathbb{J}_{b}}(\overline{X_{\mu}^{\lambda}(b)})
, which is a contradiction. So
X_{\mu}^{\lambda}(b)
is not irreducible as desired.

6.6.
Smallness implies irreducibility

Now we show
X_{\mu}^{\lambda}(b)
is irreducible if
{\lambda}
is small. We need some results on permissible vectors introduced in §
6.4
.

Let
(,):V\times V\to\mathbb{R}
be the Killing form such that
\langle\beta,\gamma^{\vee}\rangle=\frac{2(\beta^{\vee},\gamma^{\vee})}{(\beta^{\vee},\beta^{\vee})}
for
\beta,\gamma\in\Phi
. For any
p(b\sigma)
-orbit
\mathcal{O}
of
\Pi
we set
r_{\mathcal{O}}=\sum_{\xi\in\mathcal{O}}\xi
and
r_{\mathcal{O}}^{\vee}=\sum_{\xi\in\mathcal{O}}\xi^{\vee}
. Then we have the identification
r_{\mathcal{O}}=\frac{2}{(\xi^{\vee},\xi^{\vee})}r_{\mathcal{O}}^{\vee}
via the bilinear form
(,)
, where
\xi
is any/some root in
\mathcal{O}
.

Lemma 6.5
.

If
v\in V^{p(b\sigma)}_{\rm gen}\cap Y
is permissible, then
\{r_{\mathcal{O}}^{\vee};\mathcal{O}\in\Pi(v,+)/\langle p(b\sigma)\rangle\}
is a basis of
V^{p(b\sigma)}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
6.4
,
\Pi(v,+)
is a maximal proper
p(b\sigma)
-stable subset of
\Pi
. Hence
\{r_{\mathcal{O}}^{\vee};\mathcal{O}\in\Pi(v,+)/\langle p(b\sigma)\rangle\}
is linearly independent, and moreover,

|\Pi(v,+)/\langle p(b\sigma)\rangle|=|\Pi/\langle p(b\sigma)\rangle|-1=\dim V^{p(b\sigma)}.

So the statement follows.
∎

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
v\in V^{p(b\sigma)}_{\rm gen}\cap Y
be permissible. Let
\gamma
be an indecomposable root in
\Phi(v,+)
. Then there exists
\alpha\in\Pi(v,+)
such that
\gamma-\alpha\in\Phi_{M_{b}}\sqcup\{0\}
.

Proof.

Suppose
\langle\gamma,\beta^{\vee}\rangle\leqslant 0
for
\beta\in\Pi(v,+)
. Then
(r_{\mathcal{O}}^{\vee},r_{\mathcal{O}^{\prime}}^{\vee})\leqslant 0
for
\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O}^{\prime}\in(\Pi(v,+)/\langle p(b\sigma)\rangle)\cup\{\mathcal{O}_{\gamma}\}
. Thus the set

\{r_{\mathcal{O}}^{\vee};\mathcal{O}\in\Pi(v,+)/\langle p(b\sigma)\rangle\}\sqcup\{r_{\mathcal{O}_{\gamma}}^{\vee}\}\subseteq\Phi(v,+)

is linearly independent, which contradicts Lemma
6.5
.

Thus
\langle\gamma,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle>0
for some
\alpha\in\Pi(v,+)
. Notice that
\alpha
is indecomposable in
\Phi(v,+)
by Lemma
6.3
. If
\gamma=\alpha
, the statement follows. Otherwise,
\delta:=\alpha-\gamma
is also a root. Suppose
\langle\delta,v\rangle\neq 0
. Then
\alpha=\gamma+\delta
(resp.
\gamma=\alpha+(-\delta)
) is indecomposable if
\langle\delta,v\rangle>0
(resp.
\langle\delta,v\rangle<0
), which contradicts that
\alpha
and
\gamma
are indecomposable in
\Phi(v,+)
. So we have
\langle\delta,v\rangle=0
, that is,
\delta\in\Phi_{M_{b}}
as desired.
∎

Corollary 6.7
.

Let
v,v^{\prime}\in V^{p(b\sigma)}_{\rm gen}\cap Y
be permissible. Then there exists
\varepsilon\in\Omega\cap\mathbb{J}_{b}
such that
\Pi(p(\varepsilon)(v),+)=\Pi(v^{\prime},+)
and hence
\Phi(p(\varepsilon)(v),+)=\Phi(v^{\prime},+)
.

Proof.

By Proposition
6.4
, one checks (using that
G
is adjoint) that there exists
\varepsilon\in\Omega\cap\mathbb{J}_{b}
such that

\Pi(p(\varepsilon)(v),+)=p(\varepsilon)(\Pi(v,+))=\Pi(v^{\prime},+).

By replacing
v
with
p(\varepsilon)(v)
, we may assume further
\Pi(v,+)=\Pi(v^{\prime},+)
, and it remains to show
\Phi(v,+)=\Phi(v^{\prime},+)
. Otherwise, there exists an indecomposable root
\gamma
in
\Phi(v,+)
such that
\langle\gamma,v^{\prime}\rangle<0
. By Lemma
6.6
, there exists
\alpha\in\Pi(v,+)=\Pi(v^{\prime},+)
such that
\gamma-\alpha\in\Phi_{M_{b}}\sqcup\{0\}
. Hence
\langle\gamma,v^{\prime}\rangle=\langle\alpha,v^{\prime}\rangle>0
, which contradicts our assumption. So
\Phi(v,+)=\Phi(v^{\prime},+)
as desired.
∎

Proposition 6.8
.

Let
{\lambda},{\lambda}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{\mu,b}^{\rm top}
be small cocharacters such that
{\lambda}\sim{\lambda}^{\prime}
. Then
{\lambda},{\lambda}^{\prime}
are conjugate under
\Omega\cap\mathbb{J}_{b}
. In particular,
X_{\mu}^{\lambda}(b)
and
X_{\mu}^{{\lambda}^{\prime}}(b)
are conjugate by
\Omega\cap\mathbb{J}_{b}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
5.12
, there exist permissible vectors
v,v^{\prime}\in V^{p(b\sigma)}_{\rm gen}\cap Y
such that
{\lambda},{\lambda}^{\prime}
are
v
-small and
v^{\prime}
-small respectively. In particular,
v,v^{\prime}
are both permissible. By Corollary
6.7
, there exists
\varepsilon\in\Omega\cap\mathbb{J}_{b}
such that

p(\varepsilon)(\Phi(v^{\prime},+))=\Phi(p(\varepsilon)(v^{\prime}),+)=\Phi(v,+).

Thus
\varepsilon({\lambda}^{\prime})
is also
v
-small. By replacing
{\lambda}^{\prime}
with
\varepsilon({\lambda}^{\prime})
, we may assume
{\lambda},{\lambda}^{\prime}
are both
v
-small. By Lemma
5.15
, there exists
x\in\Omega_{M_{b}}\cap\mathbb{J}_{b}
such that
x({\lambda})={\lambda}^{\prime}
. It suffices to show
x\in\Omega
.

First we claim that

(a)
x({\tilde{\alpha}})
is a simple affine root for each
\alpha\in\Pi(v,+)
.

Indeed, let
\gamma=p(x)(\alpha)\in\Phi(v,+)
. As
{\lambda}
is
v
-small, we may assume
{\lambda}_{\alpha}=0
(by replacing
\alpha
by a suitable
\langle p(b\sigma)\rangle
-conjugate). Then we have

U_{x({\tilde{\alpha}})}=xU_{\tilde{\alpha}}x^{-1}=xt^{\lambda}U_{\alpha}(\mathcal{O}_{\breve{F}})t^{-{\lambda}}x^{-1}=t^{x({\lambda})}U_{\gamma}(\mathcal{O}_{\breve{F}})t^{-x({\lambda})}\subseteq I,

where the last inclusion follows from that
x({\lambda})={\lambda}^{\prime}
is
v
-small. So
x({\tilde{\alpha}})\in\tilde{\Phi}^{+}
. By Lemma
6.3
,
\alpha
is indecomposable in
\Phi(v,+)
. Hence
\gamma
is also indecomposable in
\Phi(v,+)
. Applying Lemma
6.6
we deduce that there exists
\beta\in\Pi(v,+)
such that either
\gamma=\beta
or
\gamma=\beta+\delta
for some
\delta\in\Phi_{M_{b}}
. By symmetry,
x^{-1}(\tilde{\beta})\in\tilde{\Phi}^{+}
. In the former case,
x({\tilde{\alpha}})=\tilde{\beta}+m
for some
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
. Then
{\tilde{\alpha}}=x^{-1}(\tilde{\beta})+m
, which means
m=0
since
{\tilde{\alpha}}
is simple and
x^{-1}(\tilde{\beta})\in\tilde{\Phi}^{+}
. So we have
x({\tilde{\alpha}})=\tilde{\beta}
as desired. In the latter case, we have
x({\tilde{\alpha}})=\tilde{\beta}+\tilde{\delta}+m
for some
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
. As
\delta\in\Phi_{M_{b}}
and
x\in\Omega_{M_{b}}
, we have
x^{-1}(\tilde{\delta})\in\tilde{\Phi}_{M_{b}}^{+}
. Then

{\tilde{\alpha}}=x^{-1}(\tilde{\beta})+x^{-1}(\tilde{\delta})+m,

which is a contradiction since
{\tilde{\alpha}}
is simple but
x^{-1}(\tilde{\beta}),x^{-1}(\tilde{\delta})\in\tilde{\Phi}^{+}
. Thus (a) is proved.

By (a) we see that
x
permutes the hyperplanes
H_{\mathcal{O}}
for
\mathcal{O}\in\Pi(v,+)/\langle p(b\sigma)\rangle
, where

H_{\mathcal{O}}=\{h\in V^{b\sigma};{\tilde{\alpha}}(h)=0\text{ for any/some }\alpha\in\mathcal{O}\}

whose underlining vector space is

\displaystyle V_{\mathcal{O}}

\displaystyle=\{r\in V^{p(b\sigma)};\langle\alpha,r\rangle=0\text{ for any/some }\alpha\in\mathcal{O}\}

\displaystyle=\{r\in V^{p(b\sigma)};\langle r_{\mathcal{O}},r\rangle=0=(r_{\mathcal{O}}^{\vee},r)\}.

By Lemma
6.5
, the subspaces
V_{\mathcal{O}}
are distinct and their intersection
\cap_{\mathcal{O}}V_{\mathcal{O}}
is trivial. On the other hand, as
x\in\Omega_{M_{b}}
,
p(x)
is product of reflections
s_{\alpha}
such that
\langle\alpha,v\rangle=0=\langle\alpha,V^{p(b\sigma)}\rangle
. In particular,
p(x)
acts on
V^{p(b\sigma)}
trivially, which means
x
acts on
V^{b\sigma}
as a translation by some vector
\iota\in V^{p(b\sigma)}
. Thus
x
fixes each
H_{\mathcal{O}}
and hence
\iota\in\cap_{\mathcal{O}}V_{\mathcal{O}}=\{0\}
, that is,
x
acts trivially on
V^{b\sigma}
. In particular,
x
fixes the nonempty subset
\Delta\cap V^{b\sigma}
, which means
x\in\Omega\cap\mathbb{J}_{b}
as desired.
∎

We recall the following result in
[
60
, Theorem 3.1.1]
.

Theorem 6.9
.

Let
Z
be an irreducible component of
X_{\mu}(b)
. Then the stabilizer of
Z
in
\mathbb{J}_{b}
is a parahoric subgroup of
\mathbb{J}_{b}
.

Corollary 6.10
.

Let
{\lambda}\in\mathcal{A}_{\mu,b}^{\rm top}
. Then
X_{\mu}^{\lambda}(b)
is irreducible if and only if
{\lambda}
is small.

Proof.

In view of §
6.5
, it remains to show the “if” part. Let
{\lambda}\in\mathcal{A}_{\mu,b}^{\rm top}
be small. Thanks to Theorem
6.9
, there exists
C^{\prime}\in\mathbb{J}_{b}{\rm Irr}\overline{X_{\mu}^{\lambda}(b)}
whose stabilizer in
\mathbb{J}_{b}
contains
I\cap\mathbb{J}_{b}
. Let
{\lambda}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{\mu,b}^{\rm top}
such that
C^{\prime}\in{\rm Irr}\overline{X_{\mu}^{{\lambda}^{\prime}}(b)}
. As
I\cap\mathbb{J}_{b}
fixes
C^{\prime}
, and acts transitively on
{\rm Irr}X_{\mu}^{{\lambda}^{\prime}}(b)
(by Lemma
2.9
), we see that
\overline{X_{\mu}^{{\lambda}^{\prime}}(b)}=C^{\prime}
is irreducible, and hence
{\lambda}^{\prime}
is small. Noticing that
{\lambda}\sim{\lambda}^{\prime}
, we deduce by Proposition
6.8
that
X_{\mu}^{\lambda}(b)
is also irreducible as desired.
∎

6.7.
Computation of the stabilizer

Suppose
C=\overline{X_{\mu}^{\lambda}(b)}\in{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu}(b)
with
{\lambda}
small. Notice that
\mathbb{J}_{b}
is generated by
I\cap\mathbb{J}_{b}
,
\Omega\cap\mathbb{J}_{b}
, and the longest element
{\tilde{w}}_{\alpha}
of
W_{\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{\alpha}}}
for
\alpha\in\Pi
such that
W_{\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{\alpha}}}
is finite. by definition,
I\cap\mathbb{J}_{b}\subseteq N_{\mathbb{J}_{b}}(C)
. So
N_{\mathbb{J}_{b}}(C)
is a standard parahoric subgroup of
\mathbb{J}_{b}
, and it remains to determine which
{\tilde{w}}_{\alpha}
fixes
C
. By Lemma
5.8
, either
{\lambda}_{\beta}\leqslant-1
for
\beta\in\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}
or
{\lambda}_{\beta}\geqslant 0
for
\beta\in\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}
. In the former case, we have
\alpha\notin\Pi({\lambda})
and
{\tilde{w}}_{\alpha}C\neq C
by Corollary
5.9
(2) and Lemma
5.6
. Suppose the latter case occurs. Then
\alpha\in\Pi({\lambda})
and
{\lambda}_{\beta}=0
for some
\beta\in\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}
since
{\lambda}
small. So
{\tilde{w}}C=C
by Corollary
5.9
(1). Therefore,
N_{\mathbb{J}_{b}}(C)
is the parahoric subgroup of
\mathbb{J}_{b}
generated by
I\cap\mathbb{J}_{b}
and the longest element
{\tilde{w}}_{\alpha}
of
W_{\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{\alpha}}}
for
\alpha\in\Pi({\lambda})
. Moreover,
N_{\mathbb{J}_{b}}(C)
is of maximal length by Proposition
6.4
.

Appendix A
Proof of Proposition
6.4

We assume that
b
is basic and
G
is simple and adjoint.

A.1.

For practical computation, we need to pass to the case where
G
is absolutely simple, that is, the root system
\Phi
of
G
is irreducible. By assumption,

G_{\mathcal{O}_{{\breve{F}}}}=G_{1}\times\cdots\times G_{h},

where each
G_{i}
is an absolutely simple factor of
G
and the Frobenius automorphism
\sigma
sends
G_{i}
to
G_{i-1}
for
i\in\mathbb{Z}/h\mathbb{Z}
. Let

\pi:G_{\mathcal{O}_{{\breve{F}}}}\to G_{1}

be the projection to the first factor, which induces an identification

\mathbb{J}_{b}=\mathbb{J}_{b}^{G}\cong\mathbb{J}_{\underline{b}_{1}}^{G_{1}}=\mathbb{J}_{\underline{b}_{1}},

where
\underline{b}_{1}=\pi(b\sigma(b)\cdots\sigma^{h-1}(b))\in G_{1}({\breve{F}})
and the Frobenius automorphism of
G_{1}
is given by
\sigma^{h}
.

The following lemma follows similarly as Corollary
1.6
.

Lemma A.1
.

The projection
\pi
induces a
\mathbb{J}_{b}
-equivariant map

\pi:{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu}(b)\to\sqcup_{\mu_{1}}{\rm Irr}^{\rm top}X_{\mu_{1}}^{G_{1}}(\underline{b}_{1}).

Moreover,
N_{\mathbb{J}_{b}}(C)=N_{\mathbb{J}_{\underline{b}_{1}}}(\pi(C))
for
C\in{\rm Irr}X_{\mu}(b)
.

A.2.

Now we assume
G
is absolutely simple by Lemma
A.1
. Moreover, we adopt the notation in §
6.3
. Notice that
V^{p(b\sigma)}_{\rm gen}
is an open dense subset of
V^{p(b\sigma)}
. Notice that the diagonal map gives an isomorphism
V^{p(b\sigma)}\cong(V^{d})^{p({b_{\bullet}}\sigma_{\bullet})}
.

Fix
v\in V^{p(b\sigma)}_{\rm gen}\cap Y
and let
z,M_{b},M,b_{M}
be as in §
5.2
. Notice that
b_{M}
is a superbasic element of
M({\breve{F}})
. We define
Y(v)=\{{\lambda}\in Y;{\lambda}_{\alpha}\geqslant 0,\forall\alpha\in\Phi(v,+)\}
.

The following lemma is a reformulation of Corollary
5.5
and small cocharacters in §
6.4
.

Lemma A.2
.

Let
{\lambda_{\bullet}}=({\lambda}_{1},\dots,{\lambda}_{d})\in Y^{d}
. Then we have

(1)
{\lambda_{\bullet}}\in\mathcal{A}_{{\mu_{\bullet}},{b_{\bullet}}}^{G^{d},{\rm top}}
if and only if (1)
{\lambda}_{i}\in Y(v)
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant d
and (2)
z({\lambda_{\bullet}}):=(z({\lambda}_{1}),\dots,z({\lambda}_{d}))\in\mathcal{A}_{z({\lambda}_{\bullet}),{b_{M}}_{\bullet}}^{M^{d},{\rm top}}
, where
{b_{M}}_{\bullet}=(1,\dots,1,b_{M})\in M^{d}({\breve{F}})
.

(2)
{\lambda_{\bullet}}
is
v
-small if
{\lambda_{\bullet}}\in\mathcal{A}_{{\mu_{\bullet}},{b_{\bullet}}}^{G^{d},{\rm top}}
and for each
p(b\sigma)
-orbit
\mathcal{O}
in
\Pi(v,+)
we have
({\lambda}_{i})_{\alpha}=0
for some
\alpha\in\mathcal{O}
and
1\leqslant i\leqslant d
.

A.3.

To prove Proposition
6.4
, we need some properties of permissible vectors introduced in §
6.4
. Let
M^{\prime}\supseteq T
be a Levi subgroup and let
{\lambda},\eta\in Y
. Define

\mathcal{H}_{M^{\prime}}({\lambda},\eta)=\{\gamma\in\Phi_{M^{\prime}};{\lambda}_{\gamma}\geqslant 0,\eta_{\gamma}\leqslant-1\}=-\mathcal{H}_{M^{\prime}}(\eta,{\lambda}).

Lemma A.3
.

For
{\lambda},\eta\in Y
we have

(0)
b\sigma({\lambda})_{p(b\sigma)(\gamma)}={\lambda}_{\gamma}
for
\gamma\in\Phi
;

(1)
\mathcal{H}_{M_{b}}({\lambda},\eta)\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{M_{b}}({\lambda},\chi)\cup\mathcal{H}_{M_{b}}(\chi,\eta)
for
\chi\in Y
;

(2)
z(\mathcal{H}_{M_{b}}({\lambda},\eta))=\mathcal{H}_{M}(z({\lambda}),z(\eta)))
;

(3)
p(b\sigma)(\mathcal{H}_{M_{b}}({\lambda},\eta))=\mathcal{H}_{M_{b}}(b\sigma({\lambda}),b\sigma(\eta))
.

Proof.

Note that (1) follows by definition, and (3) follows from (0) which is proved in Lemma
1.3
(3). As
z(\Phi_{M_{b}}^{+})=\Phi_{M}^{+}
, we have
z({\lambda})_{z(\alpha)}={\lambda}_{\alpha}
for
\alpha\in\Phi_{M_{b}}
, from which (2) follows.
∎

Corollary A.4
.

Let
{\lambda_{\bullet}}=({\lambda}_{1},\dots,{\lambda}_{d})\in\mathcal{A}_{{\mu_{\bullet}},{b_{\bullet}}}^{G^{d},{\rm top}}
. For
1\leqslant i\leqslant d
we have

|\mathcal{H}_{M_{b}}({\lambda}_{i},b\sigma({\lambda}_{i}))|\leqslant{\rm def}(b),

where
{\rm def}(b)
denotes the defect of
b
.

Proof.

By Lemma
A.2
(2),
z({\lambda_{\bullet}})\in\mathcal{A}_{z({\lambda}_{\bullet}),{b_{M}}_{\bullet}}^{M^{d},{\rm top}}
. Moreover,
b_{M}
is superbasic in
M({\breve{F}})
. By Lemma
A.3
we have

\displaystyle{\rm def}(b)

\displaystyle={\rm rk}_{F}(M)

\displaystyle=|\mathcal{H}_{M}(z({\lambda}_{1}),z({\lambda}_{2}))|+\cdots+|\mathcal{H}_{M}(z({\lambda}_{d-1}),z({\lambda}_{d}))|+|\mathcal{H}_{M}(z({\lambda}_{d}),z(b\sigma({\lambda}_{1})))|

\displaystyle=|\mathcal{H}_{M_{b}}({\lambda}_{1},{\lambda}_{2})|+\cdots+|\mathcal{H}_{M_{b}}({\lambda}_{d-1},{\lambda}_{d})|+|\mathcal{H}_{M_{b}}({\lambda}_{d},b\sigma({\lambda}_{1}))|

\displaystyle\geqslant|\mathcal{H}_{M_{b}}({\lambda}_{1},{\lambda}_{i})|+|\mathcal{H}_{M_{b}}({\lambda}_{i},b\sigma({\lambda}_{1}))|

\displaystyle=|\mathcal{H}_{M_{b}}(b\sigma({\lambda}_{1}),b\sigma({\lambda}_{i}))|+|\mathcal{H}_{M_{b}}({\lambda}_{i},b\sigma({\lambda}_{1}))|

\displaystyle\geqslant|\mathcal{H}_{M_{b}}({\lambda}_{i},b\sigma({\lambda}_{i}))|,

where
{\rm rk}_{F}(M)
denote the
F
-semisimple rank of
M
, and the second equality follows from Lemma
3.8
and Lemma
3.10
.
∎

For
\alpha\in\Pi(v,+)
we define

Y(v,\alpha)=\{{\lambda}\in Y(v);{\lambda}_{\alpha}=0,|\mathcal{H}_{M_{b}}({\lambda},b\sigma({\lambda}))|\leqslant{\rm def}(b)\}.

Lemma A.5
.

If
v\in V^{p(b\sigma)}_{\rm gen}\cap Y
is permissible, then we have
Y(v,\alpha)\neq\emptyset
for
\alpha\in\Pi(v,+)
.

Proof.

By assumption, there is a
v
-small cocharacter
{\lambda_{\bullet}}=({\lambda}_{1},\dots,{\lambda}_{d})
. By definition, there exists a
\langle p(b\sigma)\rangle
-conjugate
\gamma
of
\alpha
such that
({\lambda}_{i})_{\gamma}=0
for some
1\leqslant i\leqslant d
. Moreover, we have
|\mathcal{H}_{M_{b}}({\lambda}_{i},b\sigma({\lambda}_{i}))|\leqslant{\rm def}(b)
by Lemma
A.4
. So
{\lambda}_{i}\in Y(v,\gamma)
. By Lemma
A.3
,
Y(v,\gamma)
and
Y(v,\alpha)
are conjugate under
\langle b\sigma\rangle
. So the statement follows.
∎

For
\alpha,\beta\in\Phi
we write
\alpha\to\beta
if there exists a sequence
\alpha=\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{r}=\beta
of roots in
\Phi-\Phi_{M_{b}}
such that
\gamma_{i}-\gamma_{i-1}\in\Phi^{+}
is a simple root for
1\leqslant i\leqslant r
.

Lemma A.6
.

Assume
v\in V^{p(b\sigma)}_{\rm gen}\cap Y
is permissible. If
\alpha\to\beta
with
\alpha\in\Phi(v,+)
, then
\beta\in\Phi(v,+)
.

Proof.

We can assume
\beta-\alpha
is a simple root. Notice that
\langle\beta,v\rangle\neq 0
since
\beta\notin\Phi_{M_{b}}=\Phi_{M_{v}}
. If
\beta\notin\Phi(v,+)
, then
-\beta\in\Phi(v,+)
. Thus
-\beta+\alpha\in\Pi(v,+)
is decomposable in
\Phi(v,+)
, contradicting Lemma
6.3
.
∎

A.4.
The classification

Now we apply Lemma
6.3
and Lemma
A.5
to prove Proposition
6.4
when
b
is ramified, that is,
b\in\Omega
and the identity
1
are not
\sigma
-conjugate under
\Omega
(noticing that
G
is adjoint).

We argue by a case-by-case analysis on the (connected) Dynkin diagram of
\mathbb{S}_{0}
. The simple roots
\alpha_{i}
of
\Phi^{+}
are labeled as in
[
13
, §11.4]
. If the fundamental coweight
\varpi^{\vee}_{i}
of
\alpha_{i}
is minuscule, we denote by
\omega_{i}\in\Omega\cap t^{\varpi^{\vee}_{i}}W_{0}
the unique length zero element. Let
\theta>0
denote the highest root.

For classical types we fix an ambient vector space
V_{0}=\oplus_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{R}e_{i}^{\vee}
(of
\Phi^{\vee}
) and its dual
V_{0}^{\ast}=\oplus_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{R}e_{i}
together with a pairing
\langle,\rangle
between
V_{0}
and
V_{0}^{\ast}
such that
\langle e_{i},e_{j}^{\vee}\rangle=\delta_{i,j}
.

A.4.1.
Type
D_{n}

The simple roots are
\alpha_{i}=e_{i}-e_{i+1}
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant n-1
and
\alpha_{n}=e_{n-1}+e_{n}
.

Case(4.1.1):
\sigma=\textrm{id}
and
b=\omega_{1}
. Then
V^{p(b\sigma)}=\oplus_{i=2}^{n-1}\mathbb{R}e_{i}^{\vee}
and
\Phi_{M_{b}}^{+}=\{e_{1}\pm e_{n}\}
. Suppose
\alpha_{i}\in\Phi(v,+)
for some
2\leqslant i\leqslant n-2
. Then

\displaystyle\alpha_{i}=e_{i}-e_{i+1}\to e_{i}-e_{n-1}\to e_{1}-e_{n-1};

\displaystyle\alpha_{i}=e_{i}-e_{i+1}\to e_{i}-e_{n}\to e_{i}+e_{n-1}\to e_{2}+e_{n-1}.

So
e_{2}+e_{n-1},e_{1}-e_{n-1}\in\Phi(v,+)
by Lemma
A.6
. Hence

\theta=(e_{2}+e_{n-1})+(e_{1}-e_{n-1})\in\Pi(v,+)

is decomposable in
\Phi(v,+)
, contradicting Lemma
6.3
. Suppose
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{n},\alpha_{n-1}\in\Phi(v,+)
. Then we have
\alpha_{n}\to e_{2}+e_{n}
and
\alpha_{n-1}\to e_{2}-e_{n}
. Hence
e_{2}\pm e_{n}\in\Phi(v,+)
and
\theta=\alpha_{1}+(e_{2}+e_{n})+(e_{2}-e_{n})
is decomposable in
\Phi(v,+)
, a contradiction. Thus
\Pi(v,+)
equals
\Pi\setminus\{-\alpha_{1},\theta\}
or
\Pi\setminus\{-\alpha_{n-1},-\alpha_{n}\}
as desired.

Case(4.1.2):
n
is odd and
b\sigma
is of order 4. Let
m=(n-1)/2\geqslant 2
. Then we have
V^{p(b\sigma)}=\oplus_{i=2}^{m}\mathbb{R}(e_{i}^{\vee}-e_{n+1-i}^{\vee})
and

\Phi_{M_{b}}^{+}=\{e_{i}+e_{n+1-i};2\leqslant i\leqslant m\}\cup\{e_{1}\pm e_{m+1},e_{1}\pm e_{n},e_{m+1}\pm e_{n}\}.

Denote by
T\subseteq M^{1}
(resp.
T\subseteq M^{i}
for
2\leqslant i\leqslant m
) the Levi subgroup of
M_{b}
whose set of positive roots is
\{e_{1}\pm e_{m+1},e_{1}\pm e_{n},e_{m+1}\pm e_{n}\}
(resp.
\{e_{i}+e_{n+1-i}\}
).

Suppose
\alpha_{i},\alpha_{n-i}\in\Phi(v,+)
for some
2\leqslant i\leqslant m-1
. Then
m\geqslant 3
and

\displaystyle\alpha_{n-i}=e_{n-i}-e_{n-i+1}\to e_{n-i}-e_{n}\to e_{n-i}+e_{n-1}\to e_{m+1}+e_{n-1}\to e_{m+1}+e_{m+2};

\displaystyle\alpha_{i}=e_{i}-e_{i+1}\to e_{i}-e_{m+1}\to e_{2}-e_{m+1};

\displaystyle\alpha_{i}=e_{i}-e_{i+1}\to e_{i}-e_{m+2}\to e_{1}-e_{m+2}.

So
e_{m+1}+e_{m+2},e_{2}-e_{m+1},e_{1}-e_{m+2}\in\Phi(v,+)
and
\theta=(e_{m+1}+e_{m+2})+(e_{2}-e_{m+1})+(e_{1}-e_{m+2})
is decomposable, a contradiction.

Suppose
\alpha_{n}\in\Phi(v,+)
. Then
\alpha_{n}\to e_{m+2}+e_{n}
and hence
e_{m+2}+e_{n}\in\Phi(v,+)
, that is,
v(m+2)>0
as
v(n)=v(m+1)=v(1)=0
. Thus

-\alpha_{m+1}=e_{m+2}-e_{m+1},e_{m+2}\pm e_{1},e_{m+2}\pm e_{n}\in\Pi(v,+).

Let
{\lambda}\in Y(v,-\alpha_{m+1})
. Then
{\lambda}_{-\alpha_{m}}=0
and
{\lambda}_{e_{m+2}\pm e_{1}},{\lambda}_{e_{m+2}\pm e_{n}}\geqslant 0
, which means
{\lambda}(m+1)={\lambda}(m+2)
and

1-{\lambda}(m+1)\leqslant{\lambda}(1)\leqslant{\lambda}(m+1),\ 1-{\lambda}(m+1)\leqslant{\lambda}(n)\leqslant{\lambda}(m+1)-1.

It follows that
|\mathcal{H}_{M^{1}}({\lambda},b\sigma({\lambda}))|\geqslant 4
because

e_{m+1}\pm e_{1},e_{m+1}\pm e_{n}\in\mathcal{H}_{M^{1}}({\lambda},b\sigma({\lambda})).

On the other hand, one checks that
|\mathcal{H}_{M^{i}}({\lambda},b\sigma({\lambda}))|=1
for
2\leqslant i\leqslant m
. Thus

|\mathcal{H}_{M_{b}}({\lambda},b\sigma({\lambda}))|=\sum_{j=1}^{m}|\mathcal{H}_{M^{j}}({\lambda},b\sigma({\lambda}))|\geqslant m+3>m+2={\rm def}(b),

contradicting Lemma
A.4
. Thus
\Pi(v,+)=\Pi\setminus\{-\alpha_{m},-\alpha_{m+1}\}
as desired.

Case(4.1.3):
n
is even and
b\sigma
is of order 4. Let
m=n/2\geqslant 2
. Then we have
V^{p(b\sigma)}=\oplus_{i=2}^{m}\mathbb{R}(e_{i}^{\vee}-e_{n+1-i}^{\vee})
and

\Phi_{M_{b}}^{+}=\{e_{i}+e_{n+1-i};2\leqslant i\leqslant m\}\cup\{e_{1}\pm e_{n}\}.

Suppose
\alpha_{i},\alpha_{n-i}\in\Phi(v,+)
for some
2\leqslant i\leqslant m-1
. Then
m\geqslant 3
and

\displaystyle\alpha_{n-i}=e_{n-i}-e_{n-i+1}\to e_{n-i}+e_{n}\to e_{n-i}+e_{n-i+2}\to e_{m}+e_{m+2};

\displaystyle\alpha_{i}=e_{i}-e_{i+1}\to e_{2}-e_{m+2};

\displaystyle\alpha_{i}=e_{i}-e_{i+1}\to e_{1}-e_{m}.

So
e_{m}+e_{m+2},e_{2}-e_{m+2},e_{1}-e_{m}\in\Phi(v,+)
and
\theta=(e_{m}+e_{m+2})+(e_{2}-e_{m+2})+(e_{1}-e_{m})
is decomposable in
\Phi(v,+)
, a contradiction. Suppose
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{n-1},\alpha_{n}\in\Phi(v,+)
. Then
\alpha_{n-1}\to e_{2}-e_{n}
and
\alpha_{n}\to e_{2}+e_{n}
. So
e_{2}\pm e_{n}\in\Phi(v,+)
and
\theta=\alpha_{1}+(e_{2}+e_{n})+(e_{2}-e_{n})
is decomposable in
\Phi(v,+)
, a contradiction. Therefore,
\Pi(v,+)=\Pi\setminus\{-\alpha_{m}\}
as desired.

Case(4.1.4):
b\sigma
is of order 2 and
b\in\{\omega_{n-1},\omega_{n}\}
. Let
m=\lfloor n/2\rfloor\geqslant 2
. Then we have
V^{p(b\sigma)}=\oplus_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{R}(e_{i}^{\vee}-e_{n+1-i}^{\vee})
and

\Phi_{M_{b}}^{+}=\{e_{i}+e_{n+1-i}\in\Phi;1\leqslant i\leqslant m\}.

Suppose
\alpha_{i},\alpha_{n-i}\in\Phi(v,+)
for some
2\leqslant i\leqslant m
. Then

\displaystyle\alpha_{n-i}=e_{n-i}-e_{n-i+1}\to e_{n-i}+e_{n}\to e_{2}+e_{n};

\displaystyle\alpha_{i}=e_{i}-e_{i+1}\to e_{1}-e_{n}.

So
e_{2}+e_{n},e_{1}-e_{n}\in\Phi(v,+)
and
\theta=(e_{2}+e_{n})+(e_{1}-e_{n})
is decomposable in
\Phi(v,+)
, a contradiction. It is also impossible that
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{n-1},\alpha_{n}\in\Phi(v,+)
as in Case(4.1.3). Therefore, we deduce that
\Pi(v,+)
equals
\Pi\setminus\{-\alpha_{n-1},-p(b\sigma)(\alpha_{n-1})\}
or
\Pi\setminus\{-\alpha_{n},-p(b\sigma)(\alpha_{n})\}
as desired.

A.4.2.
Type
B_{n}

The simple roots are
\alpha_{i}=e_{i}-e_{i+1}
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant n-1
and
\alpha_{n}=e_{n}
. We can assume
\sigma=\textrm{id}
and
b=\omega_{1}
. In this case,
V^{p(b\sigma)}=\oplus_{i=2}^{n}\mathbb{R}e_{i}^{\vee}
and
\Phi_{M_{b}}^{+}=\{e_{1}\}
. Suppose
\alpha_{i}\in\Phi(v,+)
for some
2\leqslant i\leqslant n-1
. Then

\alpha=e_{i}-e_{i+1}\to e_{2}-e_{n}\to e_{2}\to e_{2}+e_{n}\to e_{1}+e_{n}.

So
e_{2}-e_{n},e_{1}+e_{n}\in\Phi(v,+)
and
\theta=(e_{2}-e_{n})+(e_{1}+e_{n})
is decomposable in
\Phi(v,+)
, a contradiction.

Suppose
\alpha_{1}\in\Phi(v,+)
. Then
\alpha_{1}\to e_{1}-e_{n}\in\Phi(v,+)
, which means
v(n)<0
as
v(1)=0
. So we have
-\alpha_{n}=-e_{n},\pm e_{1}-e_{n}\in\Phi(v,+)
. Let
{\lambda}\in Y(v,-\alpha_{n})
. Then
{\lambda}_{-\alpha_{n}}=0
and
{\lambda}_{\pm e_{1}-e_{n}}\geqslant 0
, which means
{\lambda}(n)=0
,
{\lambda}(1)-{\lambda}(n)\geqslant 1
and
-{\lambda}(1)-{\lambda}(n)\geqslant 0
, a contradiction. Thus
\Pi(v,+)=\Pi\setminus\{-\alpha_{n}\}
as desired.

A.4.3.
Type
C_{n}

The simple roots are
\alpha_{i}=e_{i}-e_{i+1}
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant n-1
and
\alpha_{n}=2e_{n}
. We can assume
\sigma=\textrm{id}
and
b=\omega_{n}
. Let
m=\lfloor n/2\rfloor\geqslant 1
. Then
V^{p(b\sigma)}=\oplus_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{R}(e_{i}^{\vee}-e_{n+1-i}^{\vee})
and

\Phi_{M_{b}}^{+}=\{e_{i}+e_{n+1-i};1\leqslant i\leqslant m+1\}.

Case(4.3.1):
n=2m
. Suppose
\alpha_{i},\alpha_{n-i}\in\Phi(v,+)
for some
1\leqslant i\leqslant m-1
. Then
m\geqslant 2
and

\displaystyle\alpha_{n-i}=e_{n-i}-e_{n-i+1}\to e_{n-i}+e_{n}\to e_{m}+e_{n};

\displaystyle\alpha_{i}=e_{i}-e_{i+1}\to e_{1}-e_{m}\to e_{1}-e_{n}

So
e_{m}+e_{n},e_{1}-e_{m},e_{1}-e_{n}\in\Phi(v,+)
and
\theta=(e_{m}+e_{n})+(e_{1}-e_{m})+(e_{1}-e_{n})
is decomposable in
\Phi(v,+)
, a contradiction.

Suppose
\alpha_{n},-\theta\in\Phi(v,+)
. Then
\alpha_{n}=2e_{n}\to 2e_{m+1}
and
-\theta\to-2e_{m}
, which means
2e_{m+1},-2e_{m}\in\Phi(v,+)
. Let
{\lambda}\in Y(v,-\alpha_{m})
. Then
{\lambda}_{-\alpha_{m}}=0
and
{\lambda}_{2e_{m+1}},{\lambda}_{-2e_{m}}\geqslant 0
, which means
{\lambda}(m+1)={\lambda}(m)
,
{\lambda}(m+1)>0
,
{\lambda}(m)\leqslant 0
, a contradiction. Thus
\Pi(v,+)=\Pi\setminus\{-\alpha_{m}\}
as desired.

Case(4.3.2):
n=2m+1
. Suppose
\alpha_{i},\alpha_{n-i}\in\Phi(v,+)
for some
1\leqslant i\leqslant m
. Then

\displaystyle\alpha_{n-i}=e_{n-i}-e_{n-i+1}\to e_{n-i}+e_{n}\to e_{m+1}+e_{m+2};

\displaystyle\alpha_{i}=e_{i}-e_{i+1}\to e_{1}-e_{m+1}\to e_{1}-e_{m+2}.

So
e_{m+1}+e_{m+2},e_{1}-e_{m+1},e_{1}-e_{m+2}\in\Phi(v,+)
and
\theta=(e_{m+1}+e_{m+2})+(e_{1}-e_{m+1})+(e_{1}-e_{m+2})
is decomposable in
\Phi(v,+)
, a contradiction. Thus
\Pi(v,+)=\Pi\setminus\{-\alpha_{n},\theta\}
as desired.

A.4.4.
Type
A_{n-1}

The simple roots are
\alpha_{i}=e_{i}-e_{i+1}
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant n-1
. Let
\varsigma_{0}
be the automorphism exchanging
\alpha_{i}
and
\alpha_{n-i}
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant n-1
.

Case(4.4.1):
\sigma=\textrm{id}
. Suppose
\langle b\rangle=\langle\omega_{h}\rangle
for some
1\leqslant h\leqslant n-1
dividing
n
. Then

\Phi_{M_{b}}^{+}=\{e_{i}-e_{j}\in\Phi^{+};i-j\in h\mathbb{Z}\}.

If
h=1
, then
v=0
and
\Pi(v,+)=\emptyset
as desired. Suppose
h\geqslant 2
and we can assume
\alpha_{1}\in\Pi(v,+)
. If
\alpha_{i}\in\Phi(v,+)
for some
2\leqslant i\leqslant h
, then

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}=e_{1}-e_{2}\to e_{1}-e_{i}\to e_{1}-e_{h}

\displaystyle\alpha_{i}=e_{i}-e_{i+1}\to e_{i}-e_{h+1}.

So
e_{1}-e_{h+1},e_{1}-e_{h}
and their
\langle p(b\sigma)\rangle
-conjugates are contained in
\Phi(v,+)
. Hence

\theta=(e_{1}-e_{h+1})+(e_{h+1}-e_{2h+1})+\cdots+(e_{n-2h+1}-e_{n-h+1})+(e_{n-h+1}-e_{n})

is decomposable in
\Phi(v,+)
, a contradiction. Thus
\Pi(v,+)=\Pi\setminus\mathcal{O}
, where
\mathcal{O}
is any
p(b\sigma)
-orbit of
\Pi
.

Case(4.4.2):
\sigma=\varsigma_{0}
,
b=\omega_{1}
and
n\geqslant 4
is even. Let
m=n/2\geqslant 2
. Then we have

\Phi_{M_{b}}^{+}=\{e_{1}-e_{m+1}\}.

Suppose
\alpha_{i},\alpha_{n+1-i}\in\Phi(v,+)
for some
2\leqslant i\leqslant m-1
, then
m\geqslant 3
and

\displaystyle\alpha_{i}=e_{i}-e_{i+1}\to e_{i}-e_{n+1-i}\to e_{1}-e_{n+1-i}

\displaystyle\alpha_{n+1-i}=e_{n+1-i}-e_{n+2-i}\to e_{n+1-i}-e_{n}.

So
e_{1}-e_{n+1-i},e_{n+1-i}-e_{n}\in\Phi(v,+)
and
\theta=(e_{1}-e_{n+1-i})+(e_{n+1-i}-e_{n})
is decomposable in
\Phi(v,+)
, a contradiction. Suppose
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{m}\in\Phi(v,+)
, then
\alpha_{1}\to e_{1}-e_{m}
and
\alpha_{m}\to e_{m}-e_{n}
. So
\theta=(e_{1}-e_{m})+(e_{m}-e_{n})
is decomposable in
\Phi(v,+)
, a contradiction. Thus
\Pi(v,+)
equals
\Pi\setminus\{-\alpha_{m},-\alpha_{m+1}\}
or
\Pi\setminus\{-\alpha_{1},\theta\}
as desired.

A.5.
Type
E_{6}

The simple roots
\alpha_{i}
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant 6
are labeled as in
[
13
, §11.4]
. We can assume
\sigma=\textrm{id}
and
b=\omega_{1}
. Then we have

V^{p(b\sigma)}=\mathbb{R}\alpha_{4}^{\vee}\oplus\mathbb{R}(\alpha_{2}^{\vee}+\alpha_{3}^{\vee}+2\alpha_{4}^{\vee}+\alpha_{5}^{\vee}).

Suppose
\alpha=\alpha_{i}\in\Phi(v,+)
with
i=2
or
i=4
. Let
\beta=\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{4}+\alpha_{5}
and
\gamma=\beta+\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{4}+\alpha_{6}
. Then

\alpha\to\beta\to\beta+\alpha_{4}\to\gamma.

So
\beta,\gamma\in\Phi(v,+)
and
\theta=\beta+\gamma
is decomposable in
\Phi(v,+)
, a contradiction. Thus
\Pi(v,+)=\Pi\setminus\{-\alpha_{1},-\alpha_{6},\theta\}
as desired.

A.6.
Type
E_{7}

The simple roots
\alpha_{i}
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant 7
are labeled as in
[
13
, §11.4]
. We can assume
\sigma=\textrm{id}
and
b=\omega_{7}
. Then we have

\Phi_{M_{b}}^{+}=\{\gamma+\alpha_{3},\gamma+\alpha_{5}+\alpha_{6}-\alpha_{1},\gamma+\alpha_{5}\},

where
\gamma=\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}+2\alpha_{4}+\alpha_{5}+\alpha_{6}+\alpha_{7}
. Suppose
\alpha=\alpha_{i}\in\Phi(v,+)
for some
1\leqslant i\leqslant 6
. Let
\xi=\gamma+\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{5}-\alpha_{7}
. Then
\gamma-\beta,\xi-\beta\notin\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}\Pi_{0}
for
\beta\in\Phi_{M_{b}}^{+}
, which implies that

\displaystyle\alpha\to\gamma\text{ and }\alpha\to\xi.

So
\gamma,\xi\in\Phi(v,+)
and
\theta=\gamma+\xi
is decomposable in
\Phi(v,+)
, a contradiction. Thus
\Pi(v,+)=\Pi\setminus\{-\alpha_{7},\theta\}
as desired.

Appendix B
Proof of Proposition
1.2

Let
b\in\Omega
be basic. Let
J\subseteq\mathbb{S}_{0}
be a minimal
\sigma
-stable subset such that
[b]\cap M_{J}({\breve{F}})\neq\emptyset
. Then
[b]\cap M_{J}({\breve{F}})
is a superbasic
\sigma
-conjugacy class of
M({\breve{F}})
. Suppose there is another minimal
\sigma
-stable subset
J^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbb{S}_{0}
such that
[b]\cap M_{J^{\prime}}({\breve{F}})\neq\emptyset
. To prove Lemma
1.2
, we have to show that
J=J^{\prime}
. Choose
x\in\Omega_{J}
and
x^{\prime}\in\Omega_{J^{\prime}}
such that
x,x^{\prime}\in[b]
. Let
{}^{J}W_{0}^{J}
be the set of elements
w\in W_{0}
which are minimal in its double coset
W_{J^{\prime}}wW_{J}
. For
u\in W_{0}
we set
\text{\rm supp}_{\sigma}(u)=\cup_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\sigma^{i}(\text{\rm supp}(u))\subseteq\mathbb{S}_{0}
, where
\text{\rm supp}(u)\subseteq\mathbb{S}_{0}
is the set of simple reflections that appear in some/any reduced expression of
u
.

Following
[
15
]
, we say
{\tilde{w}}\in\tilde{W}
is
\sigma
-straight if

\ell({\tilde{w}}\sigma({\tilde{w}})\cdots\sigma^{n-1}({\tilde{w}}))=n\ell({\tilde{w}})\text{ for }n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 1}.

Moreover, we say a
\sigma
-conjugacy class of
\tilde{W}
is straight if it contains some
\sigma
-straight element. By
[
15
, Proposition 3.2]
, the
\sigma
-conjugacy classes of
x
and
x^{\prime}
are straight. Moreover, as
x,x^{\prime}\in[b]
, these two straight
\sigma
-conjugacy classes coincide by
[
15
, Theorem 3.3]
. Thus there exists
{\tilde{w}}\in\tilde{W}
such that
{\tilde{w}}x=x^{\prime}\sigma({\tilde{w}})
. Write
p({\tilde{w}})=uzw^{-1}
with
u\in W_{J^{\prime}}
,
w\in W_{J}
and
z\in{}^{J^{\prime}}W_{0}^{J}
. By taking the projection
p
, we have

zw^{-1}p(x)\sigma(w)=u^{-1}p(x^{\prime})\sigma(u)\sigma(z).

Notice that
z,\sigma(z)\in{}^{J^{\prime}}W_{0}^{J}
. Moreover, we have
\text{\rm supp}_{\sigma}(w^{-1}p(x)\sigma(w))=J
and
\text{\rm supp}_{\sigma}(u^{-1}p(x^{\prime})\sigma(u))=J^{\prime}
by the minimality of
J
and
J^{\prime}
. This means that
z=\sigma(z)
and
zJz^{-1}=J^{\prime}
. So Proposition
1.2
follows from the following lemma.

Lemma B.1
.

Let
J\subseteq\mathbb{S}_{0}
be a minimal
\sigma
-stable subset such that
[b]\cap M_{J}({\breve{F}})\neq\emptyset
. If there exists
z=\sigma(z)\in W_{0}^{J}
such that
zJz^{-1}\subseteq\mathbb{S}_{0}
, then
zJz^{-1}=J
.

Proof.

It suffices to consider the case where
G=G_{\rm ad}
and
\mathbb{S}_{0}
is connected. If
b
is unramified, that is,
1\in[b]
, then we can take
J=\emptyset
and the statement is trivial. So we assume that
b
is not unramified. By the discussion above, it suffices to show the statement for some fixed
J
, and we can take
J
as follows. Let
v
be a generic point of
Y_{\mathbb{R}}^{p(b\sigma)}
, that is, if
\langle\alpha,v\rangle=0
for some
\alpha\in\Phi
, then
\langle\alpha,Y_{\mathbb{R}}^{p(b\sigma)}\rangle=0
. Then we take
J
to be the set of simple reflections
s
such that
s(\bar{v})=\bar{v}
, where
\bar{v}
is the unique dominant
W_{0}
-conjugate of
v
. Then
[b]\cap M_{J}({\breve{F}})
is a superbasic
\sigma
-conjugacy class of
M_{J}({\breve{F}})
by
[
15
, Lemma 3.1]
.

Case(1):
\mathbb{S}_{0}
is of type
A_{n-1}
for
n\geqslant 2
. Take the simple roots as
\alpha_{i}=e_{i}-e_{i+1}
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant n-1
. Let
\omega_{1}
be the generater of
\Omega\cong\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}
such that
\omega_{1}\in t^{\varpi_{1}^{\vee}}W_{0}
, where
\varpi_{1}^{\vee}
is the fundamental coweight corresponding to the simple root
\alpha_{1}
. Assume
b=\omega_{1}^{m}
for some
m\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Case(1.1):
\sigma=\textrm{id}
. Let
h
be the greatest common divisor of
m
and
n
, and
f=n/h
. Then we can take

J=\{s_{i+jf};1\leqslant i\leqslant f-1,0\leqslant j\leqslant h-1\}.

Here, and in the sequel,
s_{i}
denotes the simple reflection corresponding to the simple root
\alpha_{i}
. By assumption,
z
sends each of the subsets

D_{j}=\{1+jf,2+jf,\dots,(j+1)f\},\quad 0\leqslant j\leqslant h-1

to a subset of the form

\{k+1,k+2,\cdots k+f\}\subseteq\{1,2,\cdots,n\}.

This implies that
z
permutes the sets
D_{j}
for
0\leqslant j\leqslant h-1
. In particular,
zJz^{-1}=J
as desired.

Case(1.2):
\sigma
is of order 2. In this case,
\sigma
sends
\alpha_{i}
to
\alpha_{n-i}
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant n-1
. As
b
is not unramified,
n
is even. Moreover, we can take
b=\omega_{1}
and

J=\{s_{n/2}\}.

Noticing that
zJz^{-1}\subseteq\mathbb{S}_{0}
is
\sigma
-stable and that
s_{n/2}
is the unique simple reflection fixed by
\sigma
, we deduce that
zJz^{-1}=J
as desired.

Case(2):
\mathbb{S}_{0}
is of type
B_{n}
for
n\geqslant 2
. Then
\sigma=\textrm{id}
and
b
is of order 2 (since it is not unramified). Take the simple roots as
\alpha_{n}=e_{n}
and
\alpha_{i}=e_{i}-e_{i+1}
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant n-1
. Then we can take

J=\{s_{n}\}.

Noticing that
\alpha_{n}
is the unique short simple root, we have
z(\alpha_{n})=\alpha_{n}
and
zJz^{-1}=J
as desired.

Case(3):
\mathbb{S}_{0}
is of type
C_{n}
for
n\geqslant 3
. Then
\sigma=\textrm{id}
and
b
is of order 2. Take the simple roots as
\alpha_{n}=2e_{n}
and
\alpha_{i}=e_{i}-e_{i+1}
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant n-1
. We can take

J=\{s_{1},s_{3},\dots,s_{2\lfloor\frac{n-1}{2}\rfloor+1}\}.

If
n
is odd,
J
corresponds to the unique orthogonal subset of
(n+1)/2
simple roots, which means
zJz^{-1}=J
as desired. If
n
is even,
J
corresponds to the unique orthogonal subset of
n/2
short simple roots, which also means
zJz^{-1}=J
as desired.

Case(4):
\mathbb{S}_{0}
is of type
D_{n}
for
n\geqslant 4
. Take the simple roots as
\alpha_{n}=e_{n-1}+e_{n}
and
\alpha_{i}=e_{i}-e_{i+1}
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant n-1
. As
b
is not unramified, we have
\sigma^{2}=1
. The Weyl group
W_{0}
is the set of permutations
w
of
\{\pm 1,\dots,\pm n\}
such that
z(\pm i)=\pm z(i)
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
and
\text{ \rm sgn}(w)=0\in\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
, where

\text{ \rm sgn}(w)=|\{1\leqslant i\leqslant n;iw(i)<0\}|\mod 2.

Case(4.1):
\sigma=\textrm{id}
. If
b\in t^{\varpi_{1}^{\vee}}W_{0}
, we can take

J=\{s_{n-1},s_{n}\}.

As
zJz^{-1}\subseteq\mathbb{S}_{0}
,
z
preserves the set
\{\pm(n-1),\pm n\}
and hence
zJz^{-1}=J
as desired. If
b\in t^{\varpi_{n}^{\vee}}W_{0}
, we can take

\displaystyle J=\begin{cases}J_{1}:=\{s_{1},s_{3}\cdots,s_{n-3},s_{n-1}\},&\text{ if $n$ is even, $\frac{n}{2}$ is even; }\\
J_{2}:=\{s_{1},s_{3}\cdots,s_{n-3},s_{n}\},&\text{ if $n$ is even, $\frac{n}{2}$ is odd; }\\
J_{0}:=\{s_{1},s_{3}\cdots,s_{n-2},s_{n-1},s_{n}\},&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

Suppose
n,n/2
are even and that
J\neq zJz^{-1}\subseteq\mathbb{S}_{0}
. Then
zJz^{-1}=zJ_{1}z^{-1}=J_{2}
because
J_{1},J_{2}
correspond to the only two maximal orthogonal subset of simple roots which do not contain
\{s_{n-1},s_{n}\}
. By composing
z
with a suitable element in the symmetric group of
\{1,\dots,n\}
, we can assume that
z(\alpha_{1+2j})=\alpha_{1+2j}
for
0\leqslant j\leqslant n/2-2
and
z(\alpha_{n-1})=\alpha_{n}
. This implies that
\text{ \rm sgn}(z)=1
, which is a contradiction as desired. The case where
n
is even and
n/2
is odd follows in a similar way. Suppose
n
is odd. Then
J=J_{0}
is the unique Dynkin subdiagram of
\mathbb{S}_{0}
which is of type
(A_{1})^{\frac{n-3}{2}}\times A_{3}
. So
zJz^{-1}=J
as desired.

Case(4.1):
\sigma
is of order 2. By symmetry, we can assume
\sigma(\alpha_{n})=\alpha_{n-1}
. As
b
is not unramified, we can assume
b\in t^{\varpi_{n}^{\vee}}W_{0}
. We can take

\displaystyle J=\begin{cases}\{s_{1},s_{3}\cdots,s_{n-3},s_{n-1},s_{n}\},&\text{ if $n$ is even; }\\
\{s_{1},s_{3}\cdots,s_{n-2}\},&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

If
n
is even, then
J
corresponds to the unique orthogonal subset of
(n+2)/2
simple roots. So
zJz^{-1}=J
as desired. If
n
is odd, then
J
corresponds to the unique orthogonal
\sigma
-stable subset of
(n-1)/2
simple roots except
\alpha_{n-1}
and
\alpha_{n}
. So
zJz^{-1}=J
as desired.

Case(5):
\mathbb{S}_{0}
is of type
E_{6}
. As
b
is not unramified,
\sigma=\textrm{id}
and we can assume
b\in t^{\varpi_{1}^{\vee}}W_{0}
. Here, and in the sequel, we using the labeling of
E_{6}
and
E_{7}
as in
[
13
, §11]
. We can take

J=\{s_{1},s_{3},s_{5},s_{6}\}.

Then
zJz^{-1}=J
since
J\subseteq\mathbb{S}_{0}
is the unique Dynkin subdiagram of type
A_{2}\times A_{2}
.

Case(6):
\mathbb{S}_{0}
is of type
E_{7}
. Then
\sigma=\textrm{id}
and
b\in t^{\varpi_{7}^{\vee}}W_{0}
. We can take

J=\{s_{2},s_{5},s_{7}\}.

Then the statement is verified by computer or by the Lusztig-Spaltenstein algorithm. The proof is finished.
∎
